--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page concludes the main text of the work with a prayer-like invocation and a list of errata. The author reflects on his tone throughout the book, emphasizing humility and a desire for social harmony.</meta>

-># Conclusion<-

I have wanted to avoid certain expressions, and how many of those have I crossed out that seemed to me to contain mockery! You know that I regard even the fool <note>The author uses "fool" (German: *Thor*) not as an insult, but to describe those who are perhaps misguided or less enlightened, emphasizing their shared humanity.</note> as far too sacred for me to dare to mock him intentionally, as he is Your work as well! Fill the hearts of all men with love, tolerance, gentleness, justice, and benevolence toward one another, and let me also become a good person, a good citizen, and a useful instrument of Your workings! Amen!

# Preliminary Considerations
## regarding the foolishness and malice of those who dispute Christian doctrine.

Certainly! It must cause sincere confessors of Jesus and his divine doctrine exceedingly painful grievances when they see, in public writings, the divine authority of their Lord and Master denied, him counted among weak mortals, and his eternally valid merit disputed. If one takes a sensitive interest in the honor or belittlement shown to one's good friends, how could Christians be indifferent when their divine friend, their beloved benefactor, savior, and bringer of bliss, is publicly offered the most base dishonor instead of the honor most rightfully owed? How could they regard such an intolerable insult as a trifle, since it is shown to him who was depicted in the <term>O. T.<note>Original: "A. B." (Altes Bund/Testament). Refers to the Old Testament.</note></term> as a divine person and promised as the only savior and bringer of happiness to the sinful human race; who, through his miracles and prophecies, through his soul-saving doctrine and his incomparable virtuous conduct, proved himself to be the purpose of all prophecies and <term>prototypes<note>Original: "Vorbilder." In a theological sense, these are Old Testament figures or events seen as foreshadowing Christ.</note></term>; who was declared by God, his heavenly father, in the clouds of heaven to be the only-begotten Son of God; and who, despite all denial and disputation of his majesty, is nevertheless the Redeemer and Lord of the human race, the head of the church, the savior of repentant and faithful Christians, and the judge of all un-

<summary>This preface introduces a defense of Christ's divinity against contemporary critics, arguing that denying Jesus's divine status is a painful insult to believers and a rejection of biblical prophecy.</summary>

# Preliminary Observations
## concerning the foolishness and malice of those who dispute the Christian doctrine.

Certainly! It must cause sincere confessors of Jesus and his divine doctrine exceedingly painful grievances when they see, in public writings, the divine authority of their Lord and Master denied, him counted among weak mortals, and his eternally valid merit disputed. If one takes a sensitive interest in the honor or belittlement shown to one's good friends, how could Christians be able to remain indifferent when their divine friend, their beloved benefactor, savior, and bringer of salvation, is publicly offered the most base dishonor instead of the honor most rightfully owed? How could they be able to regard such an intolerable insult as a trifle, since it is shown to him who was depicted in the Old Testament <note>original: "A. B." (Altes Bund/Testament)</note> as a divine person and promised as the sole rescuer and bringer of happiness to the sinful human race; who, through his miracles and prophecies, through his soul-saving doctrine and his incomparable conduct of virtue, proved himself to be the goal of all prophecies and archetypes; who was declared by God, his heavenly Father, in the clouds of heaven to be the only-begotten Son of God; and who, despite all denial and disputation of his majesty, is nevertheless the Redeemer and Lord of the human race, the head of the Church, the bringer of salvation to repentant and faithful Christians, and the judge of all un-reformed

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: reformed</meta>

<summary>This introductory page expresses the deep emotional and spiritual distress felt by believers when critics deny the divinity of Jesus. It argues that Christ's status as the promised Savior is proven through biblical prophecy, miracles, and divine proclamation.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, divinity of Christ, apologetics, skepticism, Old Testament prophecy, salvation, Redeemer</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 1: The author continues a discourse on the emotional and spiritual response of believers to those who deny the divinity of Christ.</meta>

...will remain the lot of unrepentant non-Christians? — To love Jesus, and yet to see him, as it were, crucified anew through public insults, must produce very adverse feelings in a pious soul sanctified by the Spirit of God. But what are they, those short-sighted denials of the eternal divinity and the fully valid merit <note>German: "Verdienst" - referring to the theological concept of Christ's atoning work</note> of Jesus, those unwise and malicious contestations of his sublime teaching, those wanton distortions of the prophecies which were so precisely fulfilled in him, the sole purpose of creation and the Bible; what are they other than public insults to Jesus? Could one really blame Christians if they hated those—hated them as their own enemies—who degrade Jesus to the level of mere mortals, and who wish to rob them of their dearest possession, the merit of Jesus, upon which all their peace, contentment, hope, and blessedness rest? Yet Christians do not have the spirit of the world, and therefore they take care not to hate those whom the Savior himself loves, even though they are his enemies, yes, whom he once loved even unto death. Rather, they look upon all enemies of the Crucified One with compassion, lamenting their blindness and malice, and wishing them better dispositions; they pray to God for their improvement and change of heart, so that they might perhaps still escape the swift damnation and the inevitable destruction which those bring upon themselves who deny the Lord who bought them with his blood; so that they may cease leading people toward untruth and error, toward the denial of Jesus and his teaching; for truly! it remains true what Paul says in Galatians 5:10: **He who troubles the Christians shall bear his judgment, whoever he may be!**

<summary>The author argues that while Christians are deeply pained by those who deny Christ's divinity and atonement, they must respond with compassion and prayer rather than hatred. He warns that those who lead others into error regarding the faith will face divine judgment.</summary>
<keywords>divinity of Christ, atonement, religious tolerance, compassion, spiritual blindness, Paul the Apostle, Galatians 5:10, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the rise of skeptical writings; this page identifies a specific controversial work from 1783.</meta>

To the class of such misleading writings, which deny and insult Jesus, now also belongs the book which primarily prompted the present treatise, and which bears this title:

->**Horus, or an astrognostic <note>A term combining astronomy and gnosticism, suggesting that spiritual knowledge is derived from the stars.</note> final judgment on the Revelation of John and on the prophecies of the Messiah, as well as on Jesus and his disciples. — With an appendix on Europe's recent Enlightenment and on the destiny of man through God. — A reader for the recreation of scholars and a memorandum for Freemasons. — Ebenezer, Published by the House of Reason 1783.**<-

In this book, the old system of unbelief has been presented in a modern garb. Primarily, it denies the divine authority of the Bible, the divinity of Jesus, and his merit; in contrast, Jesus is turned into a worldly king, the eternity of the world is asserted, and natural religion <note>A belief system based on reason and the study of nature rather than supernatural revelation.</note> is recommended. The peculiar thing is this: that according to this work, all religions are to be derived from astrology, Preface page 12. That all folk religions <note>Commonly practiced religions of various nations.</note> flow fundamentally from a common source, from the ancient observations of celestial and natural events. Page 13. Jews, Christians, Turks <note>A common 18th-century term for Muslims.</note>, and all heathens fundamentally have only one single religion — Poor Christian religion! You who, according to your principles, are now almost six thousand years old; you who have been found proven as divine in the sharpest of trials; what must you let yourself be turned into? Now a human invention;

<summary>The author identifies a specific 1783 book titled "Horus" as a dangerous work of the Enlightenment that attempts to reduce Christianity and all other religions to mere astrological observations.</summary>
<keywords>Horus (book), Astrognosticism, Enlightenment, Rationalism, Astrology, Comparative Religion, Criticism of Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>The catchword "Horus" links this page to the previous discussion regarding the controversial work titled "Horus, or an Antidote to Christianity."</meta>

at one moment a fiction to keep the common people in check; at another a spiritual deception; and finally, in these most enlightened times, an astrological poem! a fortune-teller’s tale!

The book, like its title, has a very scholarly appearance, and initially it seems to launch very fearsome attacks against Christian doctrine. Yet the danger is not quite so great. Its author is no **Parvish** <note>Samuel Parvish, an 18th-century English deist known for his critical inquiry into the Jewish and Christian religions.</note>, no **Damm** <note>Christian Tobias Damm, a Greek scholar and theologian accused of Socinianism.</note>, no **Anonymous of Lessing** <note>A reference to Hermann Samuel Reimarus, whose radical critiques of the Bible were published anonymously by Gotthold Ephraim Lessing.</note>, who have written so learnedly and wittily against our religion; nevertheless, he possesses excellent knowledge of mathematics, astrology, and Egyptian antiquities, which one cannot deny him once one has read his book attentively. However, despite all this, he possesses the most wretched concepts of the Evangelical-Lutheran religion—to which he is said to have belonged until now and which he disputes; he knows the Bible only from a few disconnected fragments which, if I am to judge by his ***Horus***, he can never in his life have read in their proper context. Of the knowledge of human nature, ancient history, and linguistics he possesses very little, and of the rules of how to properly interpret an author, he knows nothing at all. One must therefore be exceedingly amazed how a man of thorough insights into philosophy could dare to attempt to interpret the Bible without the usual and necessary scholarly resources; yes, even more, how he can take it upon himself to alter it here and there and finally to condemn it altogether as the work of man, without sufficiently knowing or impartially examining the reasons for its divine origin.

My intention in publishing the present treatise is whether I might perhaps, in the author of the

<summary>The author critiques a controversial work titled "Horus," acknowledging the writer's skill in mathematics and Egyptology but dismissing his theological attacks as being based on a superficial understanding of the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>Horus, biblical criticism, Enlightenment, Lutheranism, Samuel Parvish, Christian Tobias Damm, Lessing's Anonymous, astrology, Egyptian antiquities</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>The catchword "kennen" from the previous page connects to the opening thought regarding the author's intentions for his readers' understanding of the faith.</meta>

**Horus** <note>The author refers to "Fragments of an Anonymous Writer," specifically the "Horus" fragment by Hermann Samuel Reimarus, which attacked traditional Christian views.</note> might awaken better dispositions toward the Christian religion and provide him with a guide to concern himself more with the reasons for, rather than against, its divinity. If only he would rather read a **Stackhouse** \*), a **Leß** \*\*), or a **Nößelt** \*\*\*), than write—against religion—himself! Next, I would like to present to readers of various kinds a proof of how firmly our religion rests upon the prophecies of the prophets, and thereby encourage them to give no credence to its enemies, or at least to be extremely suspicious of them when reading their writings. Should some, however, have already been led astray by such writings, and even by **Horus**; I would wish to lead these back to a more secure foundation. Furthermore, my readers will forgive me—if I may indeed ask for forgiveness—that I present to them here, instead of an introduction, some reflections on the lack of wisdom and the malice of those who dispute the Christian religion, since **Horus** has enticed me to these thoughts, and they may perhaps be useful to many.

It would be an unsolvable riddle why so many have written, and perhaps still write, against the beneficent doctrine of the divinity and the merit of Jesus, if one did not know the deep corruption into which human understanding has fallen, and if one were to misjudge the malice of the heart

<margin>\*) Defense of Biblical History. <note>Thomas Stackhouse (1677–1752), an English theologian known for his "New History of the Holy Bible."</note></margin>
<margin>\*\*) Proof of the Truth of the Christian Religion. <note>Gottfried Less (1736–1797), a prominent Lutheran theologian and apologist.</note></margin>
<margin>\*\*\*) Defense of the Truth and Divinity of the Christian Religion. <note>Johann August Nößelt (1734–1807), a theologian who defended Christianity against Enlightenment critiques. A large list of such writers is provided by Clemm, *Introduction to Theology*, Part I, Section 8, page 313 and following.</note></margin>

<summary>The author expresses his desire to counter the skeptical "Horus" fragments by directing readers toward established Christian apologists and the evidence of biblical prophecy. He argues that opposition to Christianity stems from a corruption of human understanding and a malicious heart.</summary>
<keywords>Apologetics, Horus Fragment, Reimarus, Stackhouse, Less, Nößelt, Prophecy, Divinity of Jesus, Enlightenment skepticism</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the origins of human corruption; this page continues that thought, identifying this corruption as the source of opposition to Christ. Catchword: Son.</meta>

# On the Enemies of Christ

...wished to recognize, which is inherited from one generation of mankind to the next. These are actually the murky sources from which such criminal behavior against Jesus <note>original: "Jesum"</note> arises. The public detractors of divine doctrine have never thought of the improvement of their understanding and heart, as the Bible commands and grants; and they are also not yet willing to work upon the same. The natural, the unrefined man declares that to be foolishness <note>original: "Thorheit"</note> which God considers wisdom. *) Must one therefore wonder where the many enemies of Jesus come from? But should one call a behavior arising from such sources "wisdom"? — Yet the opponents of Christianity <note>original: "Christenthums"</note> do not want to admit this very corruption of man. They imagine themselves far too wise, far too perfect, and this pride is exactly what prevents their improvement. Well then, we wish to consider the matter from another side; reason, in which they take so much pride, reason shall ask — speak yourselves, enemies of the Crucified One <note>original: "Gekreuzigten"</note>! What then has Jesus done to you, that you absolutely wish to deny him his majesty <note>original: "Hoheit"</note>? What urgent reasons do you have to publicly belittle him? If you have doubts in religion, why do you not allow yourselves to be instructed? Why do you turn doubts into public disputes? Are you so convinced of the truth of your dogmas that you would just as willingly undergo persecution, martyrdom, and death for their sake as the Apostles and first Christians did for the doctrine that Jesus is the eternal Son of God and the Savior of the World <note>original: "Weltheyland"</note>? Does it hinder you if there are people who—because they feel entitled and obligated to do so by irrefutable reasons—regard Jesus as the only-begotten

---

*) 1 Corinthians 2:14. <note>The verse referenced states: "The person without the Spirit does not accept the things that come from the Spirit of God but considers them foolishness."</note>

<summary>The author argues that opposition to Jesus stems from innate human corruption and intellectual pride, challenging skeptics to justify their public hostility toward Christian doctrine.</summary>
<keywords>original sin, skepticism, reason vs. faith, martyrdom, divinity of Christ, 1 Corinthians 2:14</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of the divinity and messianic role of Jesus, likely addressing skeptics or those of other faiths.</meta>

# Page 7

...worship the Son of God? Does it vex or harm you that they find and hope for their joy and bliss in him alone? Do you not recognize the virtues in Jesus that we praise in him? Do you have well-founded doubts against the history that attributes the most sublime qualities and truly divine works to Jesus? Can you in a rational manner cast doubt upon the miracles of Jesus, which antiquity—the God-fearing and learned antiquity—confirms; which Celsus <note>A 2nd-century Greek philosopher and opponent of early Christianity</note> did not dare to doubt; which the sworn enemies of Jesus, the Jews, maintain in their Talmud? Do you not see with your own eyes the success of the prophecies of Jesus, the Jews wandering about in the world? Has not their temple lain buried under ruins for more than seventeen hundred years? Could the Emperor Julian <note>Julian the Apostate, who attempted to rebuild the Temple in Jerusalem to disprove Christian prophecy</note> have it rebuilt, in defiance of the prophecy of Jesus? Can you convict him, whom we confess as our sole Master, \*) of an error in doctrine or in prophecies? Can you discover a trace in his mediatorial office <term>Mittleramt: the theological role of Jesus acting as the bridge or mediator between God and humanity</term> that is truly contrary to the perfections of God? Is it seriously impossible for you to discover therein depths—unfathomable and admirable depths—of divine wisdom and goodness, power and mercy, justice and truth? There, where the greatest men, the most acute minds, after the most mature examination, have discovered them in all ages and admired them with reverence?

If you cannot answer all these questions without blushing for shame, without being sensitively stung by your conscience, then you will have to confess that you act unwisely and maliciously when you dispute the majesty and the mediatorial office of Jesus; that you...

Everyone is curious to read a work when it appears directed against the <term>dogmas of Christianity</term>. I do not wish to investigate whether this curiosity is permissible and useful without restriction, or rather unjust, harmful, and punishable. Nor will I mention that one would do better to more diligently and generally read the Bible itself, or another useful, entertaining book containing the grounds for the Christian religion. However, I can say from experience (because I have always considered it the duty of a <term>theologian</term> <note>A scholar of divine law and scripture</note> to read and examine the writings of opponents) that one is greatly mistaken if one hopes to find much that is new in such anti-Christian books; and perhaps no one besides the divines would be moved to read them if the "coloring" <note>The rhetorical style or provocative presentation</note> did not entice them. In fact, all our opponents work toward the single point of eradicating faith in the majesty and merit of Jesus from the earth; and in this, they do nothing more than clothe the old unbelief in a new garment, or, as <term>Lessing</term> <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), a major Enlightenment figure who published controversial fragments challenging biblical literalism</note> himself admits, \*) sharpen the old arrows (against the truth) anew. In doing so, they wish to maintain the appearance of a good heart, good intentions, and commonly the pursuit of truth—as does the author of the *Horus* <note>Refers to "Adrastea oder vom Geist des Christenthums und der Religion," often associated with the "Horus" controversies of the late 18th century</note>; and thus the wolf veils himself in sheep's clothing, and Satan disguises himself as an angel of light, as the Scripture has foretold of all false teachers.

Meanwhile, one is nevertheless eager to know what reasons are actually being set against these believed and enlightened truths? — What is being set against them? What else but untruths, forced through false conclusions or through distortions of the biblical words? What else but unreasonable demands, or unpardonable contradictions, or obvious fabrications, or opinions that betray the grossest ignorance of true Christian doctrine and the interpretation of the Bible? What else but a stubborn *perhaps*, or a hasty *probably*, or a supposedly convincing *indisputably*, or a malicious: *I do not believe it.*

One need only read the *Horus* <note>The author refers to "Horus, or Final Verdict on the True Value of Christianity," a controversial deist work published in 1783 by Hermann Daniel Hermes.</note> — but no, I will not advise my readers to do that; I would rather demonstrate through a few examples from it that I have spoken the truth. I will have opportunity enough in this treatise to cite examples of false conclusions and distortions of the Bible; I will therefore begin with the unreasonable demands.

The author of *Horus* demands our approval. He wants us to believe that he is seeking truth in an honest manner, even though under this cloak he denies the main tenets of Christian doctrine, retains nothing but natural morality <term>Natural morality: the idea that ethics can be derived from human reason alone without divine revelation</term> from the Bible, and consequently speaks the language of all naturalists <term>Naturalists: thinkers who reject the supernatural and believe only in laws of nature</term>. Thus, in a concealed way, he wants to obligate us to renounce the long-accredited Bible because he rejects it without citing any capable reasons and without refuting our proofs for its divine origin; is that a reasonable demand? ...unholy, unphilosophical blunders? Truly, yes! Of the contradictions found within *Horus*, I wish to note only the following:

**page 300.** The heart of *Jesus* was entirely created for the love of mankind — His *system* <note>The critic's interpretation of Jesus's theological framework</note> commanded him to act harshly against all those who did not wish to exalt him as the King and Savior of his people.

**page 299.** Neither the Apostles nor *Jesus* were deceivers. — **page 323.** Why does he (*Jesus*) not say: "I was indeed born of a woman whom you know, but Joseph is not my father, nor is any other man; rather, God alone is my father!" Well now! He had people before him here who believed that the *Messiah* <note>original: "Messias"</note> should not be born of a woman at all. Therefore, he certainly had to claim that he was sent directly by God, and had not come into existence in the usual way. — **page 266.** The *Evangelists* <note>The authors of the four Gospels</note> — could therefore, regarding the feeding of the five and four thousand men, have either intentionally deceived the world, or have been deceived themselves by the people out of superstitious weakness.

They could have been deceived. When it comes to declaring a miracle of Jesus to be no miracle at all, the following principle is applied: a thing is possible, even if one does not immediately grasp how it is possible. However, if the subject is the incarnation and redemption of the Son of God, this principle no longer applies; then this wonderful matter, which Christians believe, is not possible. Why? Because it cannot be reconciled with their concepts of God—that is, because they do not grasp how it is possible.

One will perceive nothing but foolishness and malice in the enemies of Jesus if one further pays attention, as I said above, to their ignorance, both in pure Christian doctrine and in the interpretation of the Bible. this confession of faith was not composed by any man who possessed the extraordinary gifts of the Holy Spirit; rather, it was called "Apostolic" because it was prepared exactly according to the meaning of the writings of the Apostles. It was established as a rule by which Christians could be distinguished from non-Christians, and through which beginners in Christianity could be instructed easily and comprehensibly. Because the meaning of the divine, salvific doctrine was precisely captured in this confession, the phrase was perhaps occasionally used: "it contains God's word"; by which one meant to say: it contains the sense of the apostolic, divine teaching.

I should think I need not cite any further passages to demonstrate that the author of the **Horus** <note>A reference to 'Horus, or an Antidote to the Distrust of the Catholic Religion' (1783), a controversial work by Andreas Riem</note> is very poor in the knowledge of the true Christian religion, and perhaps also in church history. --- Page 31 ---
<meta>The catchword "wenn" (if) from the previous page connects to the opening question regarding the sincerity of a man's self-perception as a sinner.</meta>

How can an honest man, who does not wish to play the hypocrite and is otherwise in his right mind, seriously consider himself a great sinner if he is not one? Furthermore, how can someone who truly is a great sinner be called blessed, despite knowing that he is one? Should people then sin a great deal so that they might become all the more blessed? In short, I do not understand the saying.

It is a great enough shame that one wants to interpret the Bible and yet must confess their ignorance at the easiest passage—or, perhaps to put it better, is so malicious as to refuse to learn to understand the words of *Jesus*, with the shameful intention of denying and casting suspicion upon the divinity of *Jesus’s* teachings.
"Spiritually poor," or "poor in spirit," is the common designation for humility in the Bible. The Jews called this in their own language *schephal ruach* <note>original Hebrew: שפל רוח (shephal ruach), meaning humble or lowly of spirit</note>. Proverbs 29:23: "A man’s pride shall bring him low; but the poor in spirit (*schephal ruach*) shall receive honor." Proverbs 16:19: "It is better to be poor in spirit (*schephal ruach*) with the miserable than to divide the spoil with the proud." In both of these passages, being "poor in spirit" is set in opposition to pride; what else, then, can it indicate but humility? *Jesus* translates this *schephal ruach* word for word when he says: "Blessed are the poor in spirit" <note>original Greek: οἱ πτωχοὶ τῷ πνεύματι (hoi ptochoi to pneumati)</note>, the humble, or, as the author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to "Horus, or Final Judgement over the Revelation," a contemporary rationalist work by Andreas Riem</note> has copied from the German Bible, "those who are spiritually poor." What is it that *Jesus* says here that is so clumsy?

I would become too long-winded if I were to cite here all the reasons with which we prove that the kingdom of Jesus is a spiritual, invisible, and eternal kingdom. # On the Nature of the Messiah’s Kingdom

What would be the purpose of the arrangements in the Old Testament <note>The Hebrew Bible</note> that God made for a future Messiah <margin>Who this Messiah was will be shown below.</margin> if nothing more than a worldly king was to be expected in him? Would it have been worth the effort to point people toward a single person through ordinary and extraordinary teachers for thousands of years, if this person was to be no more, and achieve no more, than what any other monarch is and can achieve? Was an earthly kingdom not far too small, far too contemptible for Jesus? For him, to whom the whole world is subject? Does Jesus himself not forbid all opinions of an earthly majesty, all ideas of a worldly power? *The kingdom of God is within you* <note>original: "Das Reich Gottes ist inwendig in euch"; Luke 17:21</note>, he says; *my kingdom is not of this world* <note>John 18:36</note>. Does he not say ten times over that he came to suffer, to die, to rise from the dead; that he came to seek and to save that which is lost <note>Luke 19:10; Matthew 18:11</note>, and to give his life as a <term>redemption</term> <note>The Greek 'lytron' implies a ransom price paid for freedom</note> for the many (the lost) <note>Matthew 20:28</note>? 

Yet, let us assume the impossible case: that Jesus had a visible kingdom as his goal. Should he not have been able to reach his objective? Did not a great multitude of people gathered around him once wish to proclaim him king? What stopped him from becoming king? The bliss of flawed men is something of which we have long been convinced through a thousand strong and irrefutable reasons. One would think that it would no longer occur to a man—who ought to know and have tested the reasons why natural religion <note>Natural religion refers to a belief system based on reason and observation of the natural world, rather than on supernatural revelation or scripture.</note> is so little sufficient for the true peace of mind, security, contentment, and total welfare of faulty human beings—to praise it as the only thing that can bring happiness. Meanwhile, anyone, even the untrained, can easily see that through natural religion alone, he cannot become perfectly happy; and the whole chorus <note>original: "Chorus"; here used metaphorically to mean the collective voice of history or a group of witnesses.</note> is a proof of how miserable we would be without divine revelation.

Socrates, Aristides, Cicero, Seneca, and a hundred other examples of the most sublime men—who, despite all their wisdom and virtue, were condemned to death, banished, robbed of their property, and in other ways made miserable—all of these, I say, testify loudly enough that one can possess the most beautiful knowledge in natural religion, as did the Romans and Greeks, and yet not be happy. The history of Rome and Greece teaches that not a year passed which did not have to show such victims of cruelty and malice, of envy and pride, of greed and ambition, and of countless other vices. To deprive Christians of such a means to attain peace of mind, contentment, and consequently true **happiness**, and in its place to praise a far inferior, not universally useful, difficult, deceptive, and inadequate means to peace of mind and happiness, that is—I have said above, my readers should judge it for themselves.

The author of *Horus* <note>A reference to the 1783 book "Horus, or an Final Judgment on the Revelation of the Bible," a radical Enlightenment critique of Christianity</note> rejects, as stated, revealed religion; yet unwisely enough, he does not consider how much he, even as a human being and as a philosopher, owes to revealed religion. From where, then, do we have our enlightened concepts of God? Have we not first learned them from revelation? To be sure, we have found them to be grounded in our reason; but would we have happened upon them without revelation? Why were those wise heathens <note>Non-Christians of antiquity, specifically Greek and Roman philosophers</note> not able to arrive at such worthy ideas of the Divinity as we have? Did they not study it enough? Did they not have the same intellect, the same enlightenment, as we? *Hiero*, the tyrant, once asked *Simonides*, a learned and wise man, what God might be? The wise man asked for one day to reflect. When he was asked the following day, he asked for two more days, and subsequently, he always doubled the number of days. *Hiero*, astonished by this behavior, finally asked him why he did that? "Because," says *Simonides*, "the matter seems all the darker to me, the longer I consider it." *) Would it go any differently for us than for *Simonides*, if we had no other means to get to know God, than—

---

*) Because the matter seems to me so much more obscure, the longer I consider it. *Cicero*, Book I, *On the Nature of the Gods*, section 60. <note>original: "Quia res videtur mihi tanto obscurior, quanto diutius eam considero. Cicer. I. de nat. n. 60."</note>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>The catchword "Life" from the previous page connects to the ongoing discussion of human existence and the divine source of knowledge.</meta>

Have we had more than our own reflection? It happens to us here just as it does when we see a simple, yet previously unknown thing invented by a man. "That," we say, "I should have thought of that too! That is quite natural!" One is astonished at oneself for not having hit upon it as well; one declares one's contemporaries to be wiser and cleverer than their ancestors, who were not such fortunate inventors; one imitates the invention, it becomes a commonplace thing; one regards it, as it were, as necessary, and soon no one thinks of the inventor anymore; soon his name would be entirely forgotten if grateful history did not immortalize him. So it is with the knowledge of God. The heathens <note>original: "Heiden"; referring here to non-Christian ancient philosophers like the Greeks and Romans</note> taught contradictory nonsense about God, or confessed their ignorance; revealed religion became more general, and people obtained worthy concepts of the Deity; over time this benefit grew old, and ungratefully one now forgets the source from which this benefit was drawn.

Furthermore: From where do we have the certainty of our purpose on earth? From where do we know with reliability what is good or evil? Have not the most rational heathens often mistaken virtues for vices, and vices for virtues? From where do we know with so much certainty that our soul is a spiritual being, that it is immortal? This and much else that is important and advantageous in our knowledge was first taught to us by revelation, and it still teaches it alone in an infallible manner. One more thing: Where did the death of man come from? Did God create man for death? How could that be reconciled with the concept of a wise and benevolent Creator? To destine a creature for death, and yet give him a love for—

To instill life and the fear of death—that is cruel; such a thing cannot be imagined of God! If God had created man for the grave, then this, His favorite creature, would be worse off than the animals and the plants, and not nobler or better than they! — I cannot believe that one can demonstrate the origin of death in a rational way without the instruction of the **Bible**.

If it is shameful ingratitude to deny one's teacher, then it is certainly highly foolish and unfair, highly unjust and punishable, to reject revealed religion, since we short-sighted humans owe the most important knowledge to it.

I raised the question above: whether the enemies of the Christian religion were certainly convinced of the truth of their dogmas, which they set against Christianity? Were they so certainly convinced that they would take upon themselves persecution, martyrdom, and death as willingly for their sake as the **Apostles** and the first Christians did for the teaching that **Jesus** is the Son of God and the **Savior** <note>original: "Heiland"</note> of mankind?

# On the Rationality of Faith

# On the Vanity of Free-thinkers

Those spirits are to be marveled at who scold the faith of Christians as superstition and can boldly set themselves above it. In their opinion, it is a poor glory to repeat what even the common people profess: that Jesus is the only-begotten Son of God and the Redeemer of the sinful human race. — Those who are greedy for this vain honor should take care that they do not seek their glory in shame. Does one really need much more for the profession of a <term>Free-thinker</term> <note>German: "Freygeist"; a 18th-century term for a deist or atheist who rejects revelation</note>, next to a bold denial of the Divinity and the merit of Jesus, than impudence and over-cleverness? What a glory it is to see one's name in the register of heretics, and to place these very words into the mouths of one's rational descendants during a cold-blooded reading of that name: "This man had talents, but he used them to the shame of Him who bestowed them!" — Damned be the glory that one wishes to build upon the ruins of the honor of Jesus! May eternal shame cover the faces of those who are ashamed to profess what the prophets and apostles have professed; but are not ashamed to parrot the heathens, the free-thinkers, and the <term>Naturalists</term> <note>German: "Naturalisten"; those who believe only in natural laws and reject the supernatural or divine intervention</note>.

Every kind of human behavior has its specific consequences; unbelief as well as faith, vice as well as virtue. Should the malicious unbelief of the public challenger of the Divinity and merit of Jesus really have no unpleasant consequences? Should he be forever free from these thoughts:

> "It could indeed be true, what has always been generally believed among Christians, that Jesus is true God and the <term>Mediator</term> <note>German: "Mittler"; the theological concept of Christ acting as the bridge between God and humanity</note> between God and men! Would those first <term>martyrs</term> <note>German: "Blutzeugen"; literally "blood-witnesses"</note> and the apostles themselves have endured so much for the sake of a fiction? Should so many millions of people have been mistaken, who [relied] upon..."

...dispute divine teaching. I will now proceed to my treatise, and first provide a brief outline of it.

The Psalms are very rich sources from which we draw not only encouragement toward virtue, awakenings of devotion, and comforts in the unpleasant fates of life, but also strong arguments for the truth of Christian doctrines—especially from the excellent prophecies <note>original: "Weissagungen"</note> which they contain. The enemies of the majesty of Jesus know this well; it has therefore always been their business to declare the Psalms to be something other than prophecies. Their opinion is always based on this false conclusion:

> "There are in the Psalms, which are held to be prophecies of
> Jesus, such circumstances that fit David, or Solomon, or
> the Jewish people; consequently, the remaining circumstances,
> which are interpreted as referring to the Messiah, must also
> be understood as referring to David, or Solomon, etc."

But should one not instead make the much more certain conclusion: We find in some Psalms circumstances which—as long as the words are not maliciously twisted—can be interpreted as referring to no one else but the Messiah; consequently, the remaining circumstances, which could be understood as referring to someone else, must also be applied to the Messiah alone. For all reasonable interpreters <note>original: "Ausleger"</note> act according to these principles <note>original: "Grundsäze"</note> rightly accepted in all Psalms: A Psalm can speak of no more than one subject; and: no more than one person can speak in a Psalm. These principles are so reasonable that nothing well-founded can be objected against them; indeed, they are accepted as true even in other writings. Even our opponents quietly concede these principles—

<summary>The author introduces a treatise on the Psalms, arguing that they contain specific prophecies of Jesus that cannot be attributed to historical figures like David or Solomon. He asserts the hermeneutic principle that each Psalm has only one primary subject or speaker.</summary>

...acquiesce silently, for example, by explaining a Psalm from beginning to end as referring to David, once they have started explaining it that way. Therefore, after I have briefly examined the interpretations of the author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to the "Wolfenbüttel Fragments" or similar contemporary skeptical works often titled after Egyptian motifs of light/truth.</note> regarding the Psalms in the first chapter, I will build all the conclusions that appear in the second chapter of this treatise upon the aforementioned secure conclusion. For if I can demonstrate from the circumstances of certain Psalms that they deal neither with David nor with any other sinful mortal, it is thereby proven that they contain prophecies concerning the Messiah.

Nothing is more common than for opponents to accuse the Apostles, and those who follow them, of a false conclusion when they interpret the prophecies of the Psalms as referring to Jesus. "Here," they cry, "here one makes an obvious leap in logic! Did David name Jesus? Was he even born? <insert>See footnote below</insert>" — However, one forgets the premise: David prophesied about the Messiah, who is Jesus. What is lacking in this syllogism: David prophesied about the Messiah; Jesus is the Messiah; consequently, David prophesied about Jesus. Therefore, once I have proven the first premise—that David prophesied about the Messiah—and subsequently shown what...

...did David actually prophesy of the same? I shall demonstrate that *Jesus* is the person in whom everything has been fulfilled that David previously spoke concerning the *Messiah*. This occupies the second, third, and fourth chapters of this treatise. In the two following chapters, I intend to show how *Jesus* himself applied everything to his own person, as well as how the Apostles, following the instruction of their Master, interpreted everything as referring to him—whatever David, in a prophetic spirit, had foretold regarding the *Messiah*. In the final chapter, I shall endeavor to demonstrate how strong and cogent these proofs are for the divine authority of the person of *Jesus* and his *Messiahship*, and how one must either adopt the most absurd opinions or yield to the truth.

Furthermore, I shall concern myself only with those Psalms of David which the author of *Horus* <note>The author refers to "Horus," likely a reference to a contemporary rationalist or critical work challenging traditional biblical interpretation, possibly "Horos oder über die Sprache der Propheten" (1783) by Karl Friedrich Bahrdt.</note> has misinterpreted; not as if I were not convinced that important prophecies are contained in the remaining Psalms as well! No! Rather, I have chosen these Psalms because they are indeed the most beautiful and clearest prophecies, and because I wished to support the entire matter with various reasons—especially those which, for the most part, can be applied with little effort to the prophecies of other prophets as well. Had I intended to go through all the prophecies that the author of *Horus* has mistreated, and thoroughly prove their divinity, the present writing would have lost the appearance of a treatise <note>The author suggests the work would have become an overlong, unwieldy volume rather than a focused academic essay.</note>. Suffice it to say, I believed that the complete image of the *Messiah* provided to us by the Psalms I have examined would be sufficient to put an insolent opponent to shame.

## The First Chapter.

### The prophecies of David, which the author of the Horus has misinterpreted, with refuting notes.

I wish first of all, so that one may better oversee the matter, to name the prophecies of David <note>The Psalms were traditionally attributed to King David and viewed as prophetic of the Messiah.</note> which the author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to the controversial Enlightenment-era work "Horus, or the Final Judgment of the Clergy" (1783), often attributed to Andreas Riem.</note> has misinterpreted. I have taken the liberty of setting down his translation of the Hebrew words and the interpretations he made of them word for word, so that my readers may not accuse me of partiality when I criticize his opinion. I have taken one further liberty, namely to number the cited prophecies, so that I might refer to them more easily in the notes. They are found in the *Horus* on page 215 and following, and some which are to be found elsewhere I have united with these.

## Number 8. page 401.

> — “The Egyptians understood by the 'world' merely
> vegetative and animate nature, which they called **Horus**, or
> 'the Son of the Highest.' According to ancient Egyptian
> traditions, the father **Osiris** had begotten this son
> with the mother **Isis** from eternity, or at least from
> primeval times, and was still begetting him today. The Persians
> understood by the 'world' merely the Earth, because to them
> all celestial bodies appeared to be divine and,
> in their opinion, did not belong to the world. This
> world, according to their teachings, was likewise begotten and
> born from light and darkness—that is, from the sun and moon,
> from the God of Light and from the Goddess of Night—from
> eternity, and was still being daily begotten by them. They
> therefore called the world, or rather only the Earth, the Son
> of the Highest. But the Jews turned not only the Egyptian
> but also the Persian 'Son of the Highest' into
> their **Messiah**, as we have sufficiently seen in the
> previous chapters. From this arose that ancient
> saying: **You are my son! Today and from**
> **eternity have I begotten you.**” <note>A reference to Psalm 2:7, often interpreted in Christian theology as referring to the eternal generation of Christ.</note>

## General Remarks.

I would never reach an end if I wished to cite
everything that could be said against these interpretations. I will therefore only make a few general and
specific remarks regarding them, in order to show their
lack of foundation and the absurdity of the opinions
contained therein.

First of all, one cannot deny that
one sees prejudice in all these explanations;
that the author of *The Horus* <note>Likely referring to a specific rationalist or Enlightenment-era critique of the Bible that attempted to link Christian origins to Egyptian mythology.</note> does not grant our Bible the
justice that we are expected to show his book, in that he demands that it be read with an
unbiased mind. Namely, he was determined
to refuse his approval to the Bible and the Christian
religion derived from it; he found in
Egyptian mythology something that had some similarity with the prophecies of the prophets; and
now he already shouts the learned <term>Eureka</term> <note>original: "εύρηκα" — Greek for "I have found it!" famously attributed to Archimedes.</note>. But in
this way, I should think, one goes about it quite wrongly.
As soon as one is blinded by prejudices, one will
be unable to properly understand or correctly explain any book, no matter what kind it may be. The author
seems initially to have been willing to [dismiss] all proph—

<summary>The author critiques a rationalist work (The Horus) for its biased interpretation of the Bible and its attempts to reduce Christian prophecy to Egyptian mythology. He specifically challenges the interpretation of Jesus's final words on the cross as a loss of faith.</summary>
<keywords>Biblical criticism, Rationalism, Egyptian mythology, Jesus, Absalom, Prophecy, Prejudice, Eureka</keywords>

prophecies of the Prophets, which deal with the Messiah, as referring to the Egyptian Horus; however, because a visible similarity can be found between only a very few prophecies and Horus, he has also interpreted them as referring to other persons, such as David, Solomon, and so on. This was in accordance with his prejudice, according to which these prophecies are supposed to deal with someone else—whoever it may be—anyone but Jesus, the Messiah. And with this exceedingly partisan prejudice, he necessarily had to miss his goal of seeking the truth.

<margin>*) See Numbers 1 and Numbers 6.</margin>
The author of the *Horus* <note>Referring to a controversial Enlightenment-era work, likely "Horus, or an Examination of the Critical Value of the New Testament" (1783)</note> does not exactly offer praise to our poets when he accuses them of taking a flowery phrase they do not understand from pagan writers and placing it as a motto over their poems. Meanwhile, David is said not only to have borrowed a motto here and there from the Egyptians that he did not understand, but also to have snatched poetic adornment from them in the middle of his Psalms without understanding it *). Now, one would hardly say that of any writer who writes with sound common sense, let alone of David. What rational person will believe that this wise King acted so carelessly, so irrationally, so irresponsibly, and included in his songs—which were intended for use in public worship <term>Gottesdienste: religious services or liturgy</term>—little sayings that neither he nor the people understood? Which is more fitting? This: that David placed songs and prayers into the hands of his people that had no meaning and were taken from Egyptian idolatry <term>Abgötterey: the worship of false idols</term>,

...could have given them? Or rather this: that he instructed them through prophecies, by which they might know their future Savior and Bringer of Happiness, and their descendants might learn to distinguish him from all others? Anyone who is even slightly acquainted with the loathing that David and all true Israelites harbored against all idolatry will hardly be able to contain their laughter upon hearing the "polite" opinion that David composed songs for public worship for his people in which Egyptian gods are mentioned. By doing so, he would have made himself—at least to the greatest part of his nation—very unpopular, would have defiled the divine service, and would have acted directly contrary to the will of the true God.

And from where, then, was David supposed to have taken these Egyptian phrases? Had David learned the sciences of the Egyptians, he surely must have understood what he was doing when mentioning **Horus** <note>The Egyptian god of the sky and kingship</note> in his Psalms? Yet, no one will so easily be inclined to believe that David wanted to learn them, because the Israelites held the most terrible loathing for all pagan sciences; or that he was able to learn them, because the Egyptian priests were far too secretive, and the Egyptian people far too ignorant. For the latter not only had to believe whatever the priests made them think, but they were also constantly led astray in their religious knowledge because it was changed from time to time—partly through the various interpretations that the priests made of the <term>hieroglyphic</term> <note>A system of writing using pictures, which the author suggests were subject to the changing whims of priestly interpretation</note> figures, and partly through the introduction of the diverse religious opinions of their conquerors, and through other circumstances *).

Did David perhaps take these Egyptianisms <note>original: "Aegyptiacismos"; refers to Egyptian linguistic influences or idioms</note> from the writings of the priests? If so, the question arises: did they even have books at that time? For the stories told about Thoth or Theut—whom the Greeks call Hermes and the Latins call Mercury—are fables; and even in these fables, he is only credited with the invention of hieroglyphic figures among the Egyptians. As for the books of the younger Egyptian Hermes or Mercury (for Cicero \*) counts no more than five of them, and not all of those originated in Egypt), they were indisputably written only under the Ptolemies <note>The Greek dynasty that ruled Egypt from 305 to 30 BC</note>, when the Pythagorean-Platonic philosophy became known in Egypt—and thus over four to six hundred years after David's time \*\*).

The matter regarding Egyptian writings stands as follows: The Egyptians passed their knowledge down to posterity through hieroglyphic figures, which they engraved into pillars. The invention of these figures is said, as I mentioned before, to belong to Theut, or Thoth, or Taaut, the elder Egyptian Mercury; therefore, the pillars were also consecrated to him and were called the Pillars of Mercury. However, as the knowledge of the priests grew exceptionally in the following years, while the understanding of hieroglyphic figures remained reserved for a very few, necessity demanded that those things which the priests in their various orders needed to observe and know for the maintenance of public worship should be written down. To that end, the sacred scribes <gloss>original Greek: ἱερογραμματεῖς (hierogrammateis)</gloss> devised let-

letters, which were indeed entirely distinct from the hieroglyphic figures, but were also known only to the priests alone. They attributed these books of theirs to *Mercury* <note>The Roman name for the Egyptian god Thoth, the patron of writing and wisdom</note>; and at the time of *Clement of Alexandria* \*) forty-two such books were counted. — So relates the famous *Jablonski* \*\*) — Let us now bring this together to answer our question: *David* could not have borrowed these figures of speech from the older Egyptian *Mercury*, for the latter had invented nothing among the *Egyptians* but hieroglyphic figures; *David* would have had to know how to interpret these, and thus the author of the *Horus* <note>Likely a reference to a specific contemporary scholarly work or treatise on Egyptian mythology</note> could not say: *David* did not understand them. But that *David* did not know how to interpret them is evident because this was a secret science of the Egyptian priests; a science for which *David*, as a good *Israelite*, felt an abhorrence. Indeed, if *David* had known them and wanted to adorn his poems with them out of vanity, would he not have cited more such flourishes? Would he have repeated himself so often to his common people, and explicitly named *bread*, *wine*, and fruits of the field in his *Psalms*, if he had wanted to boast of Egyptian wisdom? *David* also could not have taken these figures of speech from the writings attributed to the younger Egyptian *Mercury*, because these books were only produced long after *David's* lifetime. And even if I were to admit, contrary to history and chronology, that some of them had already existed in *David's* time, *David* still would not have [understood] the script

understood, because the letters were of a special invention and were intelligible to the priests alone; how then would he have wanted to translate the words into the Hebrew language? — With what probability, then, can one expect to find *Egyptianisms* <note>original: "Aegyptiazismos"; refers to Egyptian linguistic influences or idioms</note> in David’s writings?

But I wish to forget all of this for now and pose another small question: Did the Egyptians at the time of David actually speak as the author of the *Horus* <note>Refers to a specific contemporary work, likely "Horus, or an Answer to the Apocalypse" by Johann Joachim Spalding or a similar rationalist critique</note> claims? He provides no proof in his book, and it seems to me that such proof would be desperately difficult for him to produce. He merely says: "my flesh shall rest in safety" means as much as: — "He drew me out of the pit and out of the mire" actually means nothing more than: — This authoritative decree—"It means as much as"—prevails throughout all these interpretations. To be sure, he excuses himself: "In the case of the prophecies concerning the *Messiah*, I was not permitted to engage in philological <note>The study of language in historical sources</note> investigations, because my book is intended to be a reader, not a reference book for *exegetes*" <note>term: Exegetes (scholars who interpret and explain biblical texts)</note>. *) Whether one might not find a middle path between a reader and a reference book for *exegetes*, I will not mention; but only this: that such philological investigations were highly necessary, because he expects that people will refute him. "Just refute my book," he says, "if it contains errors." **) An opponent, however, always demands proof; and every opponent censures this first, especially in a false teacher, when he [presents] his opinions—

# Page 59

Suppose it were granted that the words of *David* could be understood as referring to *Horus*; does it then follow that *David* spoke them regarding *Horus*? That he was talking about *Horus*? This is a very false conclusion which the author of the *Horus* <note>The author refers to a specific contemporary work, likely "Horus, or the Final Fate of Men," which attempted to reduce Christianity to Egyptian myths.</note> has bequeathed to us: The *Egyptians* spoke of *Horus* in the same way that *David* speaks here; therefore, *David* spoke of *Horus*. Two things can have the greatest similarity to one another and yet be of very different origins. Two men, to whom nature has given very similar features, expressions, and stature, are not for that reason biological brothers; just as little must all religions have sprung from a common source because each of them has something—be it more or less—similar to the other. It is historically certain that the Israelite doctrine of faith is much older than the Egyptian mythology <note>original: "Götterlehre"</note>, and visual evidence teaches that both are infinitely far apart in their main substance. How then can the former be determined by the latter? To explain the doctrine of the *Israelites* from the doctrine of the Egyptian priests would be just as foolish as if one wanted to explain the Latin language from the German, or seek the key to the interpretation of Scripture in a German *Concordance* <note>A reference book for locating words in the Bible; the author argues that using a later language/system to explain an earlier one is anachronistic.</note>—

Two writers can use the same turns of phrase here and there; who would then immediately pass judgment: they have copied one another? Or: they mean the same thing? The *Stoics* <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy.</note> asserted the *paradox* that there is only one vice, or that where one vice is present, all others are present according to their power. *James*, the apostle of *Jesus*, also says: *) If someone keeps the whole law,

The great **Isaac Newton** <margin>Isaac Newton, *Chronology of the Ancient Kingdoms*.</margin> places **Osiris** in the era of **Rehaboam** <note>Rehaboam was the son of Solomon and King of Judah; Newton attempted to synchronize biblical history with Egyptian myths.</note>, and considers him to be the same person as **Shishak**, who is mentioned in the Holy Scripture. <meta>This page continues the critique of Egyptian mythology and its application to biblical prophecy, specifically questioning the reliability of sources like Jablonski and the author of 'Horus'.</meta>

Regarding the ancient Egyptian mythology <note>original: "ägyptischen Götterlehre"</note>, which was intended to—and indeed had to—possess the appearance of profound wisdom, is it not sufficiently suited to the pride of those priests? The explanation provided by Jablonski <note>Paul Ernst Jablonski (1693–1757), a renowned scholar of Coptic and Egyptian antiquities</note> sounds quite clever and seems to harmonize best with the Egyptian way of thinking. The sun, in summer when the days are longest, attains as it were its masculine maturity and demonstrates its paternal power in various ways; in winter, however, when the days begin to increase again, it is like a child who gradually gains strength and age. Thus, the sun is both father and son, or Osiris and Horus. This Horus can also be the son of the moon, or Isis, insofar as the moon, which illuminates the dark night, precedes the rising of the sun, and the sun follows the moon just as a child follows its mother. These are the thoughts of the excellent Jablonski. <margin>See the cited work, page 220.</margin>

But to what end is all of this? Why this laborious search for the opinions of scholars regarding the time in which Osiris or Horus lived? And for the thoughts which the Egyptians may have associated with these gods? What use is it to know whether the chronologists <term>chronologists: scholars who attempt to establish historical timelines and dates</term> present these gods as ancient or recent? Or whether one should perceive the sun or the harvests in them? It is useful so that one may recognize how little certainty there is in what the author of *Horus* prattles on to us as certainty regarding this Egyptian god; how uncertain the entire ancient mythology of the Egyptians is, and how few certain conclusions can be drawn from it. It serves to convince oneself how the figures of speech in the cited prophecies of David <note>Referring to the Psalms or other biblical passages attributed to King David</note> cannot with any certainty—

## Special Observations.

### Regarding Number 1.

Since one is still uncertain, as I stated above, whether Egyptian writers even existed during the time of David? then the praise bestowed here upon our poets certainly cannot be applied to David. — The Jewish singer <note>referring to King David as the traditional author of the Psalms</note> did not understand the description of the sowing, according to the custom of all the prophets. What nonsense! Were prophets then such men who compiled into a book that which they did not understand? A fine definition of the prophets! — David is said to have regarded himself as the benefactor of his nation, and yet simultaneously spoke of Horus or the sowing; that, I should think, would be quite contradictory. And even if David had indeed wanted to speak of himself, he would surely have been able to write elegantly enough in his own language without fetching figures of speech from Egypt which he did not understand, and thus did not know whether he was insulting or praising himself through them.

— "My flesh" <note>original: "Mein Fleisch"</note> means: the grains of seed. What nation under heaven has ever thought of "flesh" and "grains of seed" as the same thing? Or used one for the other in a bold metaphor?

— "You will not leave my soul in hell" <note>A reference to Psalm 16:10</note> means merely: the life of the seed does not remain buried beneath the soil. The word "soul" (<term>Nephesh: a Hebrew term usually referring to the living being, breath, or soul</term>) does indeed often mean "life" among the Hebrews, yet only the life of humans and animals; never, however, the life of the seed or plant life; and the word "hell" (<term>Sheol: the Hebrew underworld or place of the dead</term>) nowhere signifies "the soil" to them. But even granted that "soul" could mean plant life and "hell" could mean

<summary>The author critiques a theory that interprets King David's psalms as misunderstood Egyptian metaphors for agriculture, arguing that Hebrew terms for 'soul' and 'hell' cannot be applied to plants.</summary>
<keywords>King David, Prophets, Horus, Agriculture, Seed, Soul, Nephesh, Hell, Sheol, Egypt, Biblical Criticism</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>The catchword "fung" from the previous page likely completes the word "Verwesung" (decay/corruption) which appears at the end of this page, indicating a discussion on the physical state of the body after death.</meta>

represent the soil, the question still remains: Who is praying in this passage? Or to whom is the prayer addressed? Does the seed beg its life from the sun? Does the grain fear rot? — *You will not allow your Holy One to see corruption* <note>original: "verwesen"; a reference to Psalm 16:10</note>, is supposed to mean: the seed does not rot, but merely dies in order to rise again all the more gloriously. What a multitude of untruths! **The seed does not rot**, is that true? The germ of the grain does not rot, to be sure, but the seed-grain itself certainly rots in the earth. **The seed dies and rises**; that is trying to explain metaphors with more metaphors; and what sensible exegete <note>A person who interprets or explains a text, specifically scripture.</note> would do that? Is it even correctly thought, **the seed dies and rises**? With what reason can the seed be called the "Holy One of God"? Did the Egyptians really speak this way? Did they speak so irrationally, so irresponsibly? — But we must consider this passage somewhat more precisely in the original text. My opponent translates (following Dr. Luther’s excellent translation, which is, however, not sufficient in philological <note>The study of language in oral and written historical sources.</note> disputes): **My flesh will lie secure**; but in the Hebrew it stands: **My flesh will rest in hope**. The following verse contains the reason for this hope, which Horus <note>Likely a reference to a specific opponent or a symbolic figure in the author's polemic.</note> completely forgets: **For** (*Ki*) **you will [not leave] my soul etc.** "Soul" cannot mean "life" here, for it is contrasted with the "body." It is therefore speaking of a person who has both soul and body. **Hell** (*sheol*) means, as I have already said before, never the soil in Hebrew, but rather the state of the dead, the condition of the human soul after the death of the body. Psalm 89:49. Isaiah 38:10. 1 Samuel 2:6. **Corruption** (*shachat*) does not always mean a decomposition that is already completed—

### Regarding Number 2.

I cannot find my way into the explanation: "The clothes of *Horus* were, among the *Egyptians*, allegorically the plants or the fruits of the field." <note>The author is critiquing a specific allegorical interpretation of Egyptian mythology.</note> Should it mean: "The clothes of the fruits of the field were, among the *Egyptians*, allegorically the fruits of the field"? --- Page 84 ---
<meta>Continuity established with the previous page regarding the critique of the "Horus" text; the catchword "This" (Dieß) connects to the ongoing argument about legal customs.</meta>

How the field itself can signify the "garment" of the crops is incomprehensible to me. If one were to say that the crops were the garment of the field, one might perhaps associate a sensible thought with such a metaphor. — Whether there were regular executioners <note>original: "Nachrichter"; refers to official public executioners, a role that evolved significantly in later European history</note> in David’s time? Whether the witnesses, or whoever else began the execution of a wrongdoer, always received the clothes of the executed by law or by established custom? Whether lots were always cast for them? These are questions that must first be answered in the affirmative from the study of antiquities <note>original: "Alterthümer"; the study of ancient history and customs</note> before the author of *Horus* can write so boldly: **The executioners always divided the clothes of every executed person among themselves and cast lots for them.** In the meantime, it is well enough known that the study of antiquities answers all these questions with a "No." —

<margin>\*) 1 Samuel 22:18.</margin>
The Jews never had regular executioners; rather, the witnesses, or the assembled crowd, or the court servants, or whoever was immediately at hand—as Doeg once was for Saul \*)—carried out the death sentence on the wrongdoers. Thus, Stephen was still stoned by the witnesses and by the people; Jesus, on the other hand, was crucified by soldiers according to Roman custom. — Among the Jews, the clothes were rarely, or never at all, stripped from the wrongdoers. For example, those who were stoned (which was the most common capital punishment) had their clothes pulled down only to the middle of the body, so that the stones could be thrown against the bare chest.

<summary>The author critiques a contemporary text titled "Horus," arguing against the claim that ancient Jewish executions involved professional executioners who claimed the victim's clothing by lot. He highlights the differences between Jewish stoning customs and the Roman practice of crucifixion used on Jesus.</summary>

# Page 69

**Seldenus** *) teaches us this from the Mishnah <note>The first major written collection of Jewish oral traditions</note> and from **Maimonides** <note>A highly influential 12th-century Jewish philosopher and legal scholar</note>. After the wrongdoers were put to death, they were buried together with their clothes; even the instruments of their execution—the sword, the stones, and so forth—were placed in the grave with them, so that not the slightest memory of them might remain. This is so well known that even the compendiums do not remain silent about it. **) How then can the author of **Horus** <note>Likely referring to a specific contemporary critical work or authorial pseudonym</note> say that the executioners always divided the clothes of the executed among themselves? — Regarding the casting of lots for the clothes of the executed, it is certain that the Roman soldiers cast lots for the outer garment of Jesus not out of established custom, but rather because they did not wish to tear his garment apart. But the **garment**, says **John**, ***) was seamless, woven from the top throughout. Then the soldiers said to one another: let us not tear this, but cast lots for it, to see whose it shall be. This casting of lots for the outer garment of Jesus was therefore something entirely accidental; the soldiers would not have thought of it if they had wanted to ruin the garment and make it useless, especially since the idea of casting lots only occurred to them after they had already divided the undergarments into four parts, as **John** likewise testifies in the cited passage. — Unquestionably, the author of **Horus** has committed a double error here; first, that he took this casting of lots for something general and customary at all times; second, that he

But before we leave this prophecy, we wish to take another look at the Hebrew words (*begadai* and *lebuschi* <note>original: "begadai" and "lebuschi" — my garments and my raiment</note>), which my opponent, following our Doctor Luther, translates as "clothes" and "garment." Specifically, the word *beged* <note>original: "beged" — a general term for clothing</note> signifies clothing in general, including both the outer and inner garments together. Thus, the entire high-priestly attire in Exodus 28:2, 4 is called *bigde kodesch* <note>original: "bigde kodesch" — holy garments</note>. The word *lebusch* <note>original: "lebusch" — raiment or vesture</note>, on the other hand, seems to designate the outer garment in particular. In Esther 6:8, Ahasuerus <note>The Persian King Xerxes I</note> demands that Mordecai should be clothed in the royal robe (*lebusch malchut, ascher labaschbo hammelech* <note>original: "lebusch malchut..." — the royal apparel which the king used to wear</note>). In Isaiah chapter 14:19, the prophet threatens the King in Babylon that he should remain lying in a garment of the slain and those pierced through; that is, that they would not dress him royally, but leave him lying on the battlefield among other corpses in his bloody overcoat, and finally throw him into a common pit in that same clothing. Also, the magnificent and silken garments in Ezekiel 23:6, 12 are called *lebusche'*, which were indisputably outer garments. Without doubt, therefore, *beged* and *lebusch* are to be distinguished like *genus* and *species* <note>Latin: "general category" and "specific type"</note>, and the sense in our passage seems to be: "They divide my clothes among them, yes, for my outer robe they cast lots." — How beautifully does this apply to Jesus? — But how does it apply to the "Seed" <note>A reference to the opponent's interpretation of the messianic "seed" or offspring</note>?

The phrase *bor schaon* <note>Hebrew: בּוֹר שָׁאוֹן (bor shaon), literally "pit of noise" or "roaring pit"</note> thus signifies a deep, water-rich well in which a great noise—a hollow echoing—arises as soon as a stone or something else is thrown into it. Good David! One moment you are not supposed to understand what you write; the next, you are supposed to want to find yourself in so-called Egyptian phrases; you are even supposed to take these for prophecies about yourself! Yet this is only conjectured as probable; one is not yet convinced of it by mathematically correct proofs. What could have moved David to think of his own person when using this figurative expression? One may read the reason in *Horus*, namely: because he had been made king by Samuel out of the mud of lowliness. But could David really have compared his status as a shepherd with the mud of lowliness? Were not the cattle herders the most respected people among the Israelites? Even without considering that, this interpretation of the image is against the linguistic usage. Filth and mud <note>original: "Koth und Schlamm"</note> (*did* and *javen* <note>Hebrew: טיט (tit) and יון (yaven), referring to miry clay or mud</note>) never indicate lowliness among the Hebrews when they use it as a metaphor, but rather suffering and tribulations, *Psalm* 69, verses 3 and 15. Just as great waters are likewise an image of suffering for them, *Psalm* 18, verse 17; *Psalm* 69, verses 2, 3, and 15. How then can David be speaking here of his elevation to the throne? Since his words, by virtue of linguistic usage, indicate: He rescued me from the deepest suffering, in which I must have perished without his help. How can David speak of his royal anointing, since not a trace of it is to be found in the entire Psalm? In short, how can David be thinking of himself, since the Psalm speaks of the inadequacy of sacrifices for the reconciliation of men in *Verse* 7, and hereby points to a far more perfect and greater sacrifice, which the *Messiah* himself would bring before God through his sufferings in his own person.

In the other passage: "Behold, I come, in the book it is written of me," I find once again the best Hebrew, and no Egyptian, figure of speech; but at the same time I also find that the author of *Horus* tears these words out of context and separates them from the following verse, with which they are inseparably connected. For what is written of the person speaking here in the book—

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the historical timeline of writing materials, specifically refuting the idea that King David could have used Egyptian idioms related to scrolls.</meta>

that the first books of the Egyptians were written on tree leaves, and therefore could not be rolled onto rods. Whether the Egyptians subsequently shared this custom with the Israelites? And whether they proceeded in this manner with the Egyptian reed or paper <note>original: "Papier"; referring here to papyrus</note> upon which they wrote, which was invented in Alexander's time, nearly seven hundred years after David's death? To decide that, I will leave to the antiquarians who are more learned than I am. I content myself with having shown, from the words "Scroll of the Book," that David could not have used an Egyptian turn of phrase here, even if the Egyptians had already possessed books in his time, and even if they had wound the books written on paper onto scrolls after Alexander's time. For it is a primary rule of the art of interpretation <term>Hermeneutics: the theory and methodology of interpretation, especially of scriptural texts</term>, which is based on the most well-known concepts, that one must not assume an author speaks of things that were only invented or became customary after his time; nor that the same author could have borrowed idioms from other peoples which those people themselves only introduced among themselves perhaps long after his time.

# On the Use of Musical Instruments

The distinction between the **shofar** <note>original: "Posaune (schophar)"; a ram's horn used for signaling</note> and the **trumpet** <note>original: "Trompete (chazozerah)"; a straight metal instrument</note> and their various uses among the Israelites is well known. The shofar was blown not only by the priests and Levites, but also by other people when it occurred outside the Temple. Within the Temple, however, the priests sounded the shofar only on certain festive days, and by no means every day or as often as the sun rose.

The trumpet, on the other hand—which God ordained through Moses in Numbers 10:2—was blown by the priests alone. This occurred not only in extraordinary cases, such as festivities, fasts, wars, and triumphs, but they also made themselves heard with trumpets daily, though only during the morning and evening services, just as the Levites did with other instruments (1 Chronicles 16:40, compared with verses 6 and 42).

Now the Psalmist says here: *God ascends with shouts of joy, namely Jehovah, amid the sound of the shofar.* <note>A reference to Psalm 47:5</note> Therefore, what is said in the "new interpretation" regarding the trumpeting of the Levites does not fit here at all. Even if I were to concede—contrary to the language and antiquities of the Israelites—that the Levites had trumpeted daily, this would certainly not have happened in honor of the sun. 

Was not the first law of the Israelites directed against idolatry? And against which kind of idolatry most of all? Was it not against the worship of the sun, which was most common in the Orient? Did the Israelites ever call the sun "Jehovah"? Was not **Jehovah** the proper name of the true God, by which He intended to distinguish Himself from all false gods? *) Did the Israelites ever [regard] the rising—

---

*) See the treatise on the name Jehovah by the late Senior Pastor <term>Primarius</term> <note>The head pastor of a city or district</note> at Leipzig, Doctor Christian August Crusius, translated by Master Rudolf Julius Walther, 1767.

<summary>This page discusses the ritual distinctions between the shofar and the trumpet in Israelite worship, arguing against modern interpretations that suggest these instruments were used for sun worship. The author emphasizes that the name Jehovah was strictly reserved for the one true God to prevent idolatry.</summary>

Osiris, Isis, and Horus already existed; granted, David might have felt no pangs of conscience in borrowing these figures of speech from the Egyptians; granted, this wise king might have acted so irrationally as to want to give his nation a song for use in divine service which contained phrases about idols that he himself did not understand; even assuming all this and more, the proof is still lacking that the Egyptians spoke of their gods in this manner, as the author of the *Horus* <note>Likely referring to a contemporary rationalist or "Higher Criticism" work that attempted to trace Hebrew theology back to Egyptian mythology.</note> asserts.
