poured out, especially when he teaches us to think of mice, moles, caterpillars, and the like among the heathens; in short, I do not wish to dissect this nonsense any further. I would rather briefly demonstrate that the passage of the Psalm in question is perfectly good Hebrew and contains nothing but Hebraisms <note>Hebraism: an idiom or custom specific to the Hebrew language and culture</note>, which the author of *Horus* <note>Referring to the controversial book "Horus, or an Examination of the History of the Old and New Testaments" (1783)</note> certainly did not understand. The Hebrews have at all times called the divine omnipotence the "Right Hand" or the "Right Side" (*iamin* <note>original: יָמִין</note>). Exodus 15:6, 12; Psalm 118:15, 16; Isaiah 48:13. The Jewish princes allowed those whom they wished to honor greatly to sit at their right hand. 1 Kings 2:19; Psalm 45:10. Indeed, "to sit" (*iaschab* <note>original: יָשַׁב</note>) even signifies "to reign" among them. 1 Kings 3:6 compared with 2 Chronicles 1:8; 1 Kings 1:13, 17, 20; and in this sense, they attribute sitting to God Himself. Isaiah 40:22. Accordingly, the Psalmist means to say: Jehovah said to my Lord: Take part in the honor of reigning together with me, or, let everything be subject to your power, wisdom, and justice, just as it is to mine. — What follows is again a perfect Hebraism, and actually means: until I shall have made your enemies a footstool for your feet. The Jews had the custom of treading on the necks of their conquered enemies. Joshua 10:24. From this arose the very common idiom among them: "to give under the feet." 1 Kings 5:3; Psalm 18:40; by which they expressed defeating or overcoming. Thus, the meaning here is also this: until I shall make your enemies your subjects and bondsmen. — In this passage, therefore, not a trace of an Egyptiacism <note>Egyptiacism: a term used here to describe a supposed Egyptian linguistic or cultural influence</note> is to be found. When was David, before his accession to the throne, brought to such an extreme that he would have had to say of himself: "You thrust sore at me, that I might fall"? <note>A quotation from Psalm 118:13.</note> Verse 13. ### Regarding Number 8.

Shortly before the words that fill this section, the author of the **Horus** <note>A reference to the radical Enlightenment journal "Adrastea" or similar rationalist works of the period</note> sought to retrieve and prove the long-dismissed doctrine of the eternity of the world. I will now attempt to demonstrate the true meaning of the verse he has mistreated, Psalm 2:7, based on this very proof *), since philosophy must, for once, be applied to the Bible. He writes on <term>page</term> <note>original: "pag."</note> 401: **God must have been active from eternity. Had he not been active from eternity, he would at one time not have existed, for Being and Acting are the same to him. Everything that God acts upon and has produced belongs to the world taken as a whole.** Here, in passing, we wish to note a <term>begging of the question</term> <note>original: "petitionem principii"; a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premises</note>.

---

<note>*) I hope that this proof <term>according to the man</term> <note>original: "κατ' ἀνθρωπον"; an argument adapted to the specific person being addressed</note> will not be stretched against the laws of justice, nor that erroneous thoughts regarding the mystery of the eternal generation of the Son of God will be falsely attributed to me.</note>

<summary>The author argues against a historical-only interpretation of David as the "cornerstone" and critiques a rationalist philosopher's attempt to use Psalm 2:7 to prove the world is eternal.</summary>
<keywords>David, cornerstone, Philistine, Horus, eternity of the world, Psalm 2:7, begging the question, argumentum ad hominem, generation of the Son of God</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the nature of God's actions; this page continues the philosophical and theological critique of the eternity of the world versus the eternal generation of the Son of God.</meta>

Note this. He wishes to prove the eternity of the world, and yet he also says that the effects of God, which are eternal, belong to the world. This means, unless I am very much mistaken, as much as: the world is eternal, for it has been produced from eternity. A very logical conclusion! — Now, the proposition shall be mine: God must have acted from eternity. But God has acted from eternity either within himself or outside of himself; God cannot have acted from eternity outside of himself, for otherwise he would have produced another eternal thing outside of himself, that is to say, a God. For whatever is eternal is necessary, which means I must not be able to think of it as once not having been, otherwise it would be contingent <note>The author uses "zufällig" to denote something that depends on circumstances and is not inherently necessary.</note>. Therefore, what is eternal is God. God can therefore have produced no eternal thing outside of himself, otherwise he would have produced a God outside of himself, and that is contradictory. God must therefore have acted, and still act, within himself from eternity. Holy Scripture also asserts this, and calls that which God has produced within himself from eternity the Son of God. This is precisely what the second Psalm says: God has communicated his essence to his Son from eternity, and still communicates it to him, or, as the words go: **You are my Son, today**—or, from eternity—**have I begotten you.** <note>A reference to Psalm 2:7, interpreted here through the lens of eternal generation.</note>

### Regarding Numbers 10.

How often have the words cited here from the 22nd Psalm already been mistreated by the enemies of Jesus! Should one not, if one were earnestly seeking the truth, consider for once whether these words might contain something other than the speech of a man in despair? According to Hebrew linguistic usage, this can also be expressed in the future tense. What hinders us now from translating it as: **My God, my God, how long will you forsake me?** <note>The author is arguing against the traditional past-tense "Why have you forsaken me?" (Psalm 22:1), suggesting instead a temporal plea regarding the duration of suffering.</note> The translation of the Evangelist in Matthew 27:46 <note>original: "Eli, Eli, lama sabachthani"</note> does not conflict with this at all; for this must not be judged by pure Greek language standards, but according to the habit of the New Testament writers, who write in <term>Hebraic-Greek</term> <note>Also known as Koine or Biblical Greek, heavily influenced by Hebrew syntax and thought patterns</note>. With this translation of ours, one may define "forsaking" however one wishes, yet the thought of despair will be far removed from it.

And the context demands this opinion. Already the words **My God, my God**, indicate great trust and much resignation to God; for herewith the supplicant, in his deepest sufferings, declares God to be his "good God," compare verse 11. Furthermore, in the midst of the lament, the supplicant comforts himself with God's help through the example of his ancestors (verses 5, 6), confesses with a praise of God his confidence which he has placed incessantly in God since his youth (verses 10–12), and looks forward to the complete answering of his prayer for help with the most joyful hope (verse 20 and following).

— Therefore, no other meaning can be attributed to the opening words of the Psalm than this: **My God, my God, how long will you withdraw the proofs of your love from me? How long will you let me taste and feel no evidence of your favor? How long must I be deprived of all comfort from your gracious presence?** (Psalm 13:1, 2). Are these now the thoughts of a despairing man? Is this the language used by a man who completely gives up his trust in God? Oh, it is the language of the most perfect trust. Now, however, Jesus speaks these words on the cross as he is near death;

Could his sufferings truly be the consequences of a failed attempt to establish an earthly kingdom? Could he have meant to say that he believed God would likely not rescue him now? Certainly, in such a case, he would not have quoted words from a Psalm <note>Psalm 22:1</note> in which a childlike trust in God speaks aloud—he would not have exposed his vulnerability before the crowd gathered around him—and his disciples, who honored him above all else, would they not have recounted this to posterity? Jesus would have shown some sign of sinking confidence in God already at his sentencing, or at least during the nailing to the cross—he would not have been able to utter, with his last breath, the words of the most perfect childlike trust: Father, into your hands I commit my spirit. <note>Luke 23:46</note> In short, it is impossible that the words: *My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?* <note>original: "Mein Gott, mein Gott, wie lange wirst du mich verlassen"</note> could indicate any decrease in his trust in God when Jesus allowed them to be heard.

## The Second Chapter.

### David prophesied concerning the Messiah.

By prophecies <term>Weissagungen: divine predictions of future events</term> I understand a knowledge of future contingent things which the human intellect is not capable of foreseeing or guessing by itself, and which must consequently be revealed by God *). — The predicted things are contingent events; therefore, all things belonging to the ordinary course of nature, and those which can be foreseen by men—

*) <note>Reference to Heinrich Wilhelm Clemm (1725–1775), a German theologian.</note> Clemm, *Introduction to Theology*, Vol. I, p. 433.

<summary>The author argues that Jesus's cry on the cross was an expression of faith rather than despair, then begins a new chapter defining the nature of divine prophecy in the context of King David.</summary>
<keywords>Crucifixion, Psalm 22, trust in God, Messianic prophecy, King David, definition of prophecy, Heinrich Wilhelm Clemm</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the divine nature of prophecy, specifically focusing on the Psalms of David as evidence of supernatural foresight.</meta>

previously foreseen events are excluded from this; these are not the kind of things—though they are contingent matters—that a clever mind, who knows the world from history and his own experience, could perhaps predict for another. Rather, these are far distant events, unexpected even by the wisest, and such occurrences that even higher creatures than man <note>likely referring to angels</note> could not even begin to foresee.

I would become too long-winded, and perhaps even undertake something unnecessary, if I wanted to prove in general the possibility of prophecies, their necessity, and how they are worthy of God. Who does not know that God is almighty, omniscient, and all-good? I will instead endeavor to demonstrate through several arguments that David predicted future contingent things concerning the Messiah <note>original: "Messia"</note>, which he could not have suspected or foreseen through any wisdom or cleverness, through any logical deductions, nor in any other way if left to his own devices.

Every common man, who at the very least accepts Jesus as a teacher who spoke the truth, can very quickly convince himself that David recorded events that were to happen with the Messiah long after his own lifetime. Jesus not only cites the writings of David as those having a divine origin, but also proves the truth of his own teaching—which he claims is from God, John 7:16, 17 <note>The text likely refers to John 7:16-17 where Jesus discusses his doctrine being from the Father</note>—from those very writings; on the other hand, he repeatedly rejects all human commandments in religion when facing the proud Pharisees <term>Pharisees: a Jewish sect known for strict adherence to traditional and written law</term>. Furthermore, Jesus does not want even seemingly insignificant expressions in the Psalms to be changed or criticized, such as that one, for example: *You are gods* <note>original: "ihr seyd Götter"; a reference to Psalm 82:6 cited in John 10:34-35</note>, John 10:35.

<summary>The author argues that David's prophecies regarding the Messiah were supernaturally inspired and could not have been predicted by human intellect, citing Jesus's own validation of the Psalms.</summary>
<keywords>prophecy, David, Messiah, Jesus Christ, Pharisees, divine inspiration, Psalms, Gospel of John</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "Nimmer-" (Never), likely part of a discussion regarding the reliability of biblical authors. This page continues the defense of King David's divine inspiration.</meta>

This great teacher <note>Referring to Jesus Christ</note> now, who held such a great reverence for the Psalms, expressly asserts that David, in the Spirit, calls the *Messiah* his Lord. *Matthew 22:43.* But what does "in the Spirit" mean? Indisputably this: David wrote his Psalms under the guidance and an extraordinary influence of the Spirit of God; or, in other words: the Spirit of God produced these thoughts in David's soul immediately and placed the words into his mouth; in short, it means nothing other than: David wrote his Psalms through the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. — Even this testimony of Jesus—that David was able to speak of the *Messiah* as his Lord through the immediate assistance of God—is something the author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to "Fragments of an Unnamed Author," specifically the "Wolfenbüttel Fragments" published by Lessing, which attacked traditional biblical authority</note> will not be able to entirely evade, since he at least considers *Jesus* to be a great teacher and clears him of all deceit.

But we must push this proof somewhat further. *Jesus* says: *David* spoke of the *Messiah* in the Spirit, and *David* also asserts this of himself in *2 Samuel 23:2*:

->The Spirit of the Lord has spoken through me, and His speech has been upon my tongue.<-

Is this explanation of *Jesus’s* words not clear enough? Here *David* indicates the source from which he had drawn the news of all that was to happen with the *Messiah* long after his own life. Would one be unjust enough not to grant *David* as much fairness as one grants to every other writer when he states where he obtained his information? If one still had the desire to doubt the testimony of *David*, an upright and credible man, one would at least refrain from doing so because the King spoke these words shortly before his death.

<summary>The author argues for the divine inspiration of King David's Psalms, citing Jesus's testimony in the Gospel of Matthew and David's own final words to refute Enlightenment critiques of biblical prophecy.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Inspiration, King David, Messiah, Holy Spirit, Gospel of Matthew, Book of Samuel, Biblical Criticism, Wolfenbüttel Fragments</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the divine inspiration of King David’s prophecies, specifically focusing on his role as a vessel for the Holy Spirit.</meta>

One can never declare him—an old man so close to his grave—capable of such extraordinary malice that he would have wished to attribute a higher origin to his words than they actually possessed. — Now, if David claims that the Spirit of the Lord spoke through him; if Jesus, the most sublime teacher, testifies that David spoke of the Messiah in the Spirit; then, as long as I regard these two persons even merely as pious men, I will certainly—and to say the least, with the greatest probability—be able to assert: David prophesied of the Messiah.

If one compares David's own testimony cited from 2 Samuel 23:2 with other passages in his Psalms, the same conclusion emerges. He says, for example, in Psalm 45:2, **"My tongue is the pen of a ready writer."** Indisputably, he intends to convey by this that he was only the instrument in the preparation of this Psalm, and that it has another and higher origin, having by no means been devised by the monarch through his own reflection. Another time David says: **"In the hidden part, you make wisdom known to me,"** Psalm 51:8. David is addressing God here, as the context indicates. **"In the hidden part"** means, without doubt, in a concealed manner that not only escapes everyone's notice but is also in itself inexplicable. By **wisdom**, he understands the doctrine of reconciliation, <note>The author refers to the theological concept of Atonement or the restoration of the relationship between God and humanity.</note> \*) which was to be established through the Messiah; for it was precisely therein that he sought and found comfort in the poignant memory

The conviction of the apostles regarding the truth that Jesus of Nazareth was the Messiah rested largely upon the prophecies of the prophets, among whom they included David. \*) They found in the person and life circumstances of Jesus, and in all the events that occurred involving him, the image that those writers of the Old Testament <note>original: "A. B." (Altes Bund/Testament)</note> had drafted; from this they concluded: Jesus is the person who was depicted in advance by those writers. No one who has read their writings will deny that these were the thoughts of the apostles. Now, however, the apostles endured the worst hatred and the most violent persecutions from their opponents while spreading the doctrine: Jesus is the promised Messiah; to make this doctrine known, they not only abandoned their peace and comfort, their friends and worldly goods, and everything that was dear to them, but also took upon themselves, for the sake of this doctrine, the most shameful and painful death with the most sublime steadfastness, and with the greatest patience and joy.

— I am certainly not mistaken when I say: they endured all of this hardship largely because they considered the writings of the prophets, David not excluded, to be divine, and were convinced that they had prophesied concerning the Messiah. Had they not believed these prophecies, they could not have held Jesus to be the promised Messiah, and consequently would not have subjected themselves to these heavy tribulations.

no one can be fundamentally blamed. — Now, however, one finds in the Psalms of *David* certain *predicates* <note>In logic, a predicate is a property or quality attributed to a subject.</note> that fit neither this King himself, nor any other mortal of his time; consequently, these *predicates* must define a *subject* who was to live after *David’s* time. Furthermore, one finds in these same Psalms *predicates* which fit *David* just as well as the future person of whom the former spoke; yet both types of predicates—those that did not fit *David*, and those that can be understood to refer to *David* as well as to another—stand in a single sequence. It is therefore certain that the *subject* announced by the former predicates cannot have become someone else in the latter type of predicates than he was in the first; and he can be no other in the first type than the one he can remain in the latter type. Hereby, all the words of a Psalm containing a passage that cannot apply to *David* are declared to be words which have their aim set upon the *Messiah*, even if they could just as easily be understood as referring to *David*. Assuming this to be the case, I believe I can safely make this conclusion: David speaks in the eight cited Psalms (for I restrict myself to these for the sake of brevity) either of himself or of the Messiah; now, however, David does not speak of himself, consequently his words are a depiction of the Messiah, who was still to come in his days. — I wish to support the second premise—that David did not speak of himself—with two reasons: 1) because in these Psalms many circumstances do not fit David (nor any other person of his time); 2) because many prophets (of whom it can be proven that they could boast of the very same thing that David claimed for himself in the above-cited words of 2 Samuel 23:2 <note>original: "The Spirit of the Lord speaks through me..."</note>) continued to point toward a future person long after David's time, using the exact same expressions that David used in these Psalms.

I believe the circumstances I have cited thus far from the Psalms prove the proposition strongly enough: David did not speak of his own person in these Psalms. The second proof of this proposition shall now be illustrated with several examples, which is as follows:

The prophets, long after David's time, continued to point toward a future person using the exact same expressions that David used in these Psalms.

The following passages shall serve as examples of this:

We read in Psalm 2:6, **"I have set my King upon Zion, the mountain of my holiness."** Zechariah, who lived over 500 years after David, still promises this King in Chapter 9:9, **"Rejoice, O daughter of Zion — your King comes to you."**

The suffering and its outcome, just as David depicted both regarding a future person, are still prophesied as pertaining to a future person by Isaiah, who lived over three and a half hundred <note>350</note> years after David. One may compare the following passages: Psalm 22:7, **"I am — a mockery of men and despised by the people."** Isaiah 53:3, **"He was so despised that men hid their faces from him."** — Psalm 22:12, **"Be not far from me, for anguish is near."** Isaiah 53:8, **"He was taken from anguish and from judgment."** See Psalm 118:5. — Psalm 22:31, **"He shall have a seed that serves him; it shall be told of the Lord to children's children."** Isaiah 53:10, **"He shall have seed, and live long, and the Lord's purpose shall prosper through his hand."** Zechariah also shows this glorious outcome of suffering in 8:22.

# The Third Chapter.

### What did David prophesy concerning the Messiah?

I have already reminded the reader above that the Psalms of David are to be considered in themselves as a certain whole, in which a single person, who was then still in the future, is depicted. Let us now examine more closely what the holy poet made known in advance regarding this person? — For the sake of clarity, I wish to explain the following three propositions, once again drawing from the eight cited Psalms. The first: the Messiah is not a mere human being, but at the same time true God. The second: the Messiah

*) See above, Chapter 1, Note to Number 2.

<note>The author uses "holy poet" <term>Dichter</term> to refer to David as the divinely inspired author of the Psalms.</note>

<summary>This page argues that David's prophecies in the Psalms regarding the Messiah's suffering and eventual triumph could only have been divinely inspired. It introduces a new chapter focused on proving the Messiah's divinity and dual nature as both man and God.</summary>
<keywords>Messiah, King David, Prophecy, Psalms, Divinity of Christ, Biblical Interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 107: The author continues the theological examination of the Messiah's nature as revealed through the Psalms of David.</meta>

The Messiah is Priest, Teacher, and King. The third point: the Messiah is distinguished from all other people on the earth.

According to the cited prophecies of David, the Messiah is a **God-man** <note>German: *Gottmensch*; a theological term referring to the union of divine and human natures in Christ</note>. David teaches his humanity by describing his heavy sufferings and actual death in Psalm 16:10, Psalm 22, Psalm 40:13, and Psalm 118:10–14. He calls men the brothers of the Messiah in Psalm 22:23 and bestows upon him the name **Man** in Psalm 8:5. A human being consists of body and soul; David ascribes both to the Messiah in Psalm 16:10.

However, according to David’s depiction, the Messiah could not be a flawed, sinful human being. How sublime are the virtues that David praises in him? How heroic is the obedience toward God that he attributes to him in Psalm 40:9? How great is the eulogy when he calls him the **Holy One of God** in Psalm 16:10? <note>original: "Heiligen Gottes"</note> In short, the image that David sketches of the Messiah shows such a perfectly holy person that one would consider him something divine for that reason alone, even if David did not explicitly teach this. How great, how noble, according to this image, is the philanthropy <note>German: *Menschenliebe*; literally "love for humanity"</note> of the Messiah! How divine is his zeal for the glory of God! How charming is his resignation to the divine will! His childlike humiliation before God in his suffering; his languishing prayer for help, his patience, his trust in merciful Omnipotence—which he does not give up even in the most difficult struggle—must move every sensitive heart, even to tears! Could we fail to love a man who always had God before his eyes (Psalm 16:8) and whose commandments were in his heart (Psalm 40:9)? A man who desired liberation from his sufferings only so that he might all the sooner make known the glory of the Lord (Psalm 22...

22. 23. Could we consider such a man to be a sinner?

According to the prophecies of David, the Messiah was to be no mere human, but also at the same time true God. He describes him as the Son of God, to whom God communicates His essence from eternity, Psalm 2:7 <note>original: "Ps. 2, 7." The author refers to the verse: "You are my Son; today I have begotten you."</note>. One cannot form such a conception of any created being. Is it possible that God can communicate His essence to a creature? No! For God cannot do contradictory things! To have received the Divine essence means to be God. — The angels are, by their nature, exalted far above humans, and their intellectual powers—greatly heightened through long experience and practice—and their perfected holiness and virtue can never be compared with those of humans. Now, however, David says in Psalm 8:6 <note>original: "Ps. 8, 6." In modern numbering, this is usually Psalm 8:5: "You made him a little lower than the angels."</note> that the Messiah would be lowered beneath the angels for a short time; consequently, he must previously have been greater than the angels. We know, however, of no higher or more perfect creature of God than the angels; consequently, the Messiah must be higher and more perfect than all creatures. — No human can become as great as, or even greater than, the angels *). But David says in this passage that the Messiah, after his humiliation beneath the angels, would again be crowned with his former glory—that is, be raised above the angels; consequently, he must be more than a mere human. — David was an independent king. Nevertheless, he calls the Messiah, who was to descend from his late posterity, his Lord, Psalm 110:1. If the latter were a mere human, merely the descendant of David according to the flesh, and had no higher origin

My second point was this: The Messiah is Priest, Teacher, and King. — Regarding the priesthood of the Messiah, I wish to examine the exceedingly important prophecy of Psalm 110:4 <note>original: "Pf. 110, 4."</note>, where three circumstances are to be well considered: 1) that God confirmed the same with an oath; 2) that the Messiah should be a priest for eternity; and indeed 3) according to the order of Melchizedek. — *The Lord has sworn,* says David, *and will not repent.* God did not swear in regard to the priesthood according to the order of Aaron, but rather in consideration of the priesthood of the Messiah; partly because it was something new and might perhaps seem incredible, since God had already established another—namely, a covenant of eternal priesthood in Numbers 25:13 <note>original: "4 Mos. 25, 13."</note>—and had promised it to Aaron along with his descendants; and partly so that this priesthood might be established upon better promises, Hebrews 8:6 <note>original: "Hebr. 8, 6."</note>, and in such a manner be made secure and irrevocable. The priesthood of the Messiah must therefore be of the utmost importance, for otherwise the promise of it would not have been given with such a solemn confirmation by an oath. — The Messiah was to be a priest, in the proper

# XII

sense of the word, as he is also promised in Zechariah 6:13. He therefore had to offer an atoning sacrifice <note>original: "Versöhnopfer"</note>. However, David rejects (speaking in the name of the Messiah, whom he introduces as the speaker) in Psalm 40:7 the sufficiency of the sacrifices as they were offered by the Levitical priests <note>The Levitical priesthood refers to the descendants of Levi who performed temple duties under Mosaic Law</note>, and yet he mentions no other animal sacrifice that the Messiah would bring; on the contrary, he describes the innocent sufferings and an unmerited death of the Messiah; he describes the most perfect holiness of the Messiah, and yet also attributes sins to him in Psalm 40:13. These sins, by virtue of that depiction of his virtue, can be none other than imputed <note>original: "zugerechnete"; a theological term meaning assigned to someone else's account</note> sins—that is, those which the Messiah, as the guarantor <term>Bürge: a legal surety or bondsman who takes on the debt of another</term> of mankind, would take upon himself. Thus, to the sacrifice which the High Priest brought annually for the people, the sin of all Israel was imputed; i.e., the death which this innocent animal suffers (so the Israelites were meant to think) was what we deserved; but since a stranger <note>referring here to the sacrificial animal as a substitute</note> suffers the same in our place, we are free. Likewise, to every other sin-offering or burnt-offering, the sin of the one who brought it was imputed, which was signified by the laying on of hands, Leviticus 4:4, 33; Leviticus 1:4. If we take all of this together, it follows that the Messiah was to be, in his own person, the sacrifice that provides satisfaction <note>original: "genugthuende"; the concept of "atonement" or "repayment" for a debt</note> for the sins of men. Now this sacrifice, like the priesthood, was to be eternal in its duration—that is, of eternal validity—without needing to be repeated like the Levitical sacrifice. The Messiah, therefore, was not to bring a sacrifice whose value and effectiveness would one day cease; he could not be a priest like the priests according to the order of Aaron, who died and made room for others; he had to die, but he could not...

Melchizedek possessed many advantages over all the priests of the Israelites; the Messiah was, according to the prophecy of David, to be a priest after the order of Melchizedek; how many advantages, therefore, does the royal prophet hereby attribute to him over all the Levitical priests? Among the latter, their lineage had to be known, the father being from the tribe of Levi and the mother an Israelite woman. But of Melchizedek, neither his father, nor his mother, nor his lineage is recorded; indeed, mention is made of neither his birth nor his death. This did not happen without cause, but rather with the intention of depicting the unceasing duration of the priesthood of Christ. For in a mere human being, this depiction of eternity could occur in no more fitting way than if one knew nothing of his beginning and end, and received no report of either his predecessor or successor in office. —

Melchizedek, that incomparable priest, was not from the tribe of Levi; the Messiah, therefore, also did not have to be from this tribe, and consequently his sacrifice was not to be a Levitical one. — Melchizedek was king and priest at the same time, Genesis 14:18 <note>original: "1 Mos. 14, 18." referring to the First Book of Moses.</note>, and this dignity was never interrupted. How far did this advantage elevate him above the Levitical priests? This very same advantage belonged to the Lord Messiah, who was to be and remain priest and sovereign <term>Selbstherrscher: an independent ruler or autocrat, emphasizing Christ's absolute authority</term> at the same time. — Whoever accepts the tithe <note>A tenth part of one's produce or income given as a religious offering.</note> has an advantage over the one who gives it. For "anointing" is used in reference to priests (1 Chronicles 29:22), prophets (1 Kings 19:16, 19, 20), and kings (Judges 9:8, 15; 2 Samuel 2:4, 7), and signifies nothing other than: appointing, installing, and consecrating them to their office and dignity. The God-man <note>original: "Gottmensch" — a theological term referring to Christ possessing both divine and human natures</note> therefore possesses his offices in a very special way. He did not acquire them through inheritance from other kings and priests; he did not become a teacher through the determination of his own powers; he attained none of these dignities through the election of the people. Instead, he possessed all three through the special and extraordinary appointment of the Lord, and for all three he also received superhuman powers.

My third proposition was: the Messiah distinguishes himself from all other people on earth. My meaning is: in our Psalms, David makes such characteristics of the Messiah known that people could not be mistaken in his person when he appeared in the flesh. To this belongs not only what we have already considered regarding the humanity, virtue, priesthood, teaching office, and kingdom of the Messiah; but David also instructs us with very specific markers by which the God-man should make himself recognizable. Among these, the following are the most prominent: the general uprising, especially of the great men among the people, against the Messiah and his kingdom (Psalm 2:1–3; Psalm 22:13, 14; Psalm 118:10–12); that the Church of Christ, or his spiritual kingdom, should first be planted in Zion and thereafter be spread throughout the whole world (Psalm 2:6, 8; Psalm 110:2). The devotion of children to the God-man, whereby his enemies would be shamed and many would be moved to transition into his kingdom (Psalm 8:3; Psalm 118:25, 26).

## Chapter Four.

### All the characteristics that David specified regarding the Messiah are to be found in Jesus of Nazareth.

Never have Christians been less mistaken than when they regarded Jesus of Nazareth as the person whose image David designed in a prophetic spirit. For in this Jesus, all the circumstances that David foretold of the Messiah have been fulfilled so exactly and so punctually that one cannot marvel enough at those who fail to recognize this exalted person. Let us only briefly compare the marks that David made known concerning the Messiah with the events that occurred with Jesus; certainly, we shall find a most striking agreement, as noted by the author of the *Horus* <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary theological work or periodical of the Enlightenment era</note>. We do not need to make a long and laborious task of this; we only need to cast a glance back at what we have discussed so far from the Psalms. Then, with a slight recollection of the history of Jesus, the thought will occur to us quite naturally: this is exactly what happened to our Jesus as well; he was precisely the person David depicted as the Messiah.

<summary>This page concludes a list of Messianic prophecies from the Psalms and begins Chapter Four, which argues that Jesus of Nazareth perfectly fulfills every prophetic detail established by King David.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus of Nazareth, King David, Messiah, Prophecy, Psalms, Christology, Fulfillment</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 117: The text continues the theological exploration of Christ's nature, transitioning from his divinity to his physical humanity and moral perfection.</meta>

Jesus was a true human being. After his death, his body was laid into the earth. On the cross, he commended his human soul to the care of his Father. Throughout his entire life, he exhibited human—yet faultless—weaknesses. He made use of daily nourishment, sought to refresh his body through sleep, and expressed his tender heart through tears; in short, he acted like any other man in these matters.

The character, holiness, and virtue which Jesus possessed and practiced elevated him so far above all mortals that I do not dare to draft an image of them, for I fear bringing more shadow than light into a painting of this kind. How is it possible to perfectly express an original model of virtue that the world saw only once? With great emphasis, Paul <note>The Apostle Paul, likely referencing Hebrews 7:26</note> knew how to say of him: *he was holy, innocent, undefiled, separated from sinners, and higher than the heavens.* <note>original: "er war heilig, unschuldig, unbefleckt, von den Sündern abgesondert, höher, als der Himmel ist"</note> Never could the brilliance of his innocence be darkened by his cunning enemies! Neither before the people, nor before Pilate, could they awaken a doubt against his wisdom and virtue! Even their groundless accusation before the pagan judge, their impetuous shouting, their hateful injustice in choosing to grant life to a murderer <note>A reference to Barabbas</note> rather than to their beneficent prophet; the false witnesses they set up, the acquittal by the governor—everything testifies to the innocence of Jesus! And how was it otherwise possible? A life spent so holily as the life of Jesus could not be confused with the conduct of a miscreant. Had men remained silent at his execution; had men not provided proof of Jesus's innocence through the monuments established by their own malice, then God, whom—

I might therefore say, the stones themselves would have had to speak of this. — Only behold, Christians, only behold your Jesus! How inextinguishable was his zeal for the honor of God! **I honor my Father; I do not seek my own honor** <note>original: "Ich ehre meinen Vater; ich suche nicht meine Ehre"</note> \*) ; these were his watchwords! How heroic was his obedience, the obedience even unto death on the cross! How fervent and zealous was he in his prayer, how strong in his resignation to the divine will! In his deepest soul-struggle he prays:

> My Father! if it be possible, let this saddest hour of my life pass quickly, yet not my will, but Thine, be done!

As venerable as his humility before God and men made him, just as worthy of imitation is he in his patience, which he proved steadfastly even then, when—with the consciousness of his own innocence and his well-spent life—he went toward the most shameful death of the worst evildoer! How incomparable was his trust in God, which he combined with those virtues! One might perhaps have more reason to demand proof of his opinion from the author of *Horus* than he has reason to demand this of us. Meanwhile, it seems to me that the following reasons are important enough to convince us that Jesus was nailed through the feet as well as through the hands. Firstly, it was never customary among the Romans ***) to nail a criminal who was being crucified through the hands and tie him by the feet; rather, they were either nailed through both hands and feet, or else—which happened more rarely—tied by both hands and feet. <meta>This page continues the theological argument regarding the physical reality of Jesus' resurrection, specifically focusing on the evidence of his wounds as proof against the theory of a "sham death" or swoon.</meta>

# On the Physical Resurrection

Jesus was killed by the Romans in the Roman fashion. Furthermore, in order to convince his disciples that he was not a spirit, and to assure them of the truth of his resurrection—specifically in the very same unique body in which he had suffered—Jesus made use not only of the scars in his hands and his side, but also of his feet. Luke 24:39–40:

-> "See my hands and my feet, that it is I myself. Touch me and see, for a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have." <-

And as he said this, he showed them his hands and his feet. Certainly, it would have been in vain to convince his disciples by showing his feet that he was their beloved friend who had hung on the cross and had risen again in that same body—or, as he expressed it, that it was "he himself"—if they had not been able to see and touch the wounds in his feet <margin>Nonnus: "bearing iron points in both feet and palms." <note>original Greek: καὶ ποσὶ καὶ παλάμῃσι σιδήρεα κέντρα κομίζων; original Latin: et pedibus manibusque ferens stimulos ferratos. Nonnus of Panopolis was a 5th-century Greek poet who wrote a poetic paraphrase of the Gospel of John.</note></margin>. If one compares this passage from Luke with John 20:20, 25, 27 and Matthew 28:9, my argument receives even greater weight.

The matter is of even greater importance when the author of *The Horus* <note>A reference to "Wolfenbüttel Fragments" or similar Enlightenment-era skeptical literature that questioned the supernatural elements of the Bible.</note> attempts to cast doubt on the death of Jesus in order to thwart the fulfillment of the prophecy in Psalm 16:10 <note>"For thou wilt not leave my soul in hell; neither wilt thou suffer thine Holy One to see corruption."</note>. He says namely: <margin>page 347</margin> that Damm <note>Christian Tobias Damm (1699–1778), a German theologian and classicist known for his rationalist interpretations.</note> likely comes closest to the truth when he says:

> "Jesus was not entirely dead, but only in a deep swoon when he was buried."

This doubt, improbable in itself, is supported by not a single piece of historical evidence...

and then, such a reputation surely does not have the standing to represent the place of history. What the author chatters about regarding Quervell's wounds, which Kaltschmid <note>Karl Friedrich Kaltschmid (1706–1769), a German physician known for his work on anatomy and pathology.</note> claims to have healed; what he tells us of the "novelty" that not every stab and every shot is lethal; what he asserts about the wounds of Jesus—that they were not lethal, including the side-wound <note>original: "Lendenstich" (loin-stab). The author is critiquing a specific anatomical interpretation of the spear thrust.</note> (for he imagines that the Roman soldier stabbed Jesus in the loin to find out if he were dead)—all of this does not actually belong to the matter at hand. Here we are speaking of a <term>matter of fact</term> <note>original Latin: "re facti"</note> where no proofs other than historical ones can be valid. — All four Evangelists say that Jesus died, and indeed they express themselves with different words; with such words that indicate death not only in their own language, but also in all other languages. Matthew says *): Jesus cried out again loudly and <note>original Greek: ἀφῆκε τὸ πνεῦμα</note> gave up the spirit, or let the spirit depart from him. Mark and Luke **) say <note>original Greek: ἐξέπνευσε</note> he expired, he died. The soldiers did not break his legs because they saw that he was already <note>original Greek: τεθνηκότα</note> dead. †) Since the authors of the life-history of Jesus say with such clear words that Jesus died, that his soul had left the body, one must either deny the truth of this history — and thus one would have to assert that no history in the world is true —

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "Man" (One), leading into the argument that one must either accept the historical credibility of Jesus's life or admit he was truly dead.</meta>

for the history of Jesus has at least as much evidence in its favor as any other credible history has—or one must admit that Jesus was truly dead.

John mentions a very remarkable circumstance which occurred at the opening of Jesus’s side: namely, that blood and water came out of this side wound. However we may view this historical detail, it is a certain proof of the death of Jesus, because blood does not clot, and the watery part does not separate from the clotted blood, until a person has actually died. <note>The author is applying 18th-century medical understanding to the biblical account to prove the physical reality of death.</note> John also presents this as an eyewitness, with an emphatic assurance that it is true. John 19:34, 35. — The piercing of the loin-flesh <note>original: "Lendenfleisch"</note> does not seem as probable to me as it appeared to the author of *Horus*. <note>A reference to "Horus," a rationalist work by Karl Friedrich Bahrdt which attempted to provide naturalistic explanations for biblical miracles.</note> I do not know whether a flow of water, mixed with clotted pieces of blood, could be caused by such a wound; but this I know, that <term>pleura <note>original Greek: πλευρὰ</note></term> signifies the side where the ribs are located, and I believe this: that the soldier did not investigate where a stab would hurt more intensely, but rather—according to the notions of common people who look only toward the heart to see if it still moves, etc.—he stabbed toward the heart. One could therefore also very probably take the outflow of water as a sign that the sac which surrounds the heart <note>The pericardium.</note>, and in which a watery fluid collects in the dying, had been pierced with the spear; and in this case, Jesus would not have survived such a wound, even if he had still been lively and strong.

<summary>The author argues for the physical reality of Jesus's death by analyzing the biblical account of the spear wound, suggesting the "blood and water" proves death through the medical separation of fluids or the piercing of the pericardium.</summary>
<keywords>Crucifixion, medical evidence of death, Gospel of John, spear wound, pericardium, rationalist theology, Karl Friedrich Bahrdt</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 129: The author continues the argument regarding the physical reality of Jesus' death on the cross, specifically refuting the "swoon theory" (the idea that he merely fainted).</meta>

One should now consider the following historical circumstances: The soldier pierces the side of Jesus with a spear to see if he were truly dead; would they not have broken his legs if the slightest sign of a faint <note>The German "Ohnmacht" refers to a state of unconsciousness or a swoon, often used by 18th-century rationalists to suggest Jesus did not actually die on the cross.</note> had been found in him? But the guard considers all further violence to be superfluous; consequently, they were convinced of the death of Jesus. — The Senate <note>Referring here to the Sanhedrin, the supreme council and tribunal of the Jews.</note>, which absolutely demanded the death of Jesus, had also condemned him to it, and would certainly have inflicted it upon him cruelly enough if they had been permitted to do so \*) would in no way have been satisfied if Jesus had only been deeply unconscious when he was buried. These raging enemies of Jesus would have pressed Pilate for the breaking of his legs just as impetuously as they had forced his death sentence, if they had not known for certain that he was dead! It cannot be presumed otherwise than that they inquired very closely into the matter, since they were so anxious and sealed the tomb of Jesus. — The death of Jesus was an event that occurred before so many thousands of witnesses; would not at least some among them have been able to distinguish a faint from actual dying, publicly doubted the death of Jesus, informed the Senate of it, and encouraged the guard to break his legs? We find, however, no trace in history that this occurred; there must, therefore, have been the most certain signs of death, and no mark of a faint, present in Jesus, from which a general conviction of his fatal passing arose. — The councilors, who the

## The Fifth Chapter.
### Jesus interprets David's prophecies as referring to himself

I have hitherto proven that Jesus of Nazareth, the crucified and risen one, is the person of whom David prophesied in the cited Psalms. Now, however, a double question arises; the first: did Jesus also consider himself to be this person? That is, did Jesus believe and teach that David prophesied of him, and of no other Messiah? The other: did Jesus, when he interpreted the Davidic prophecies as referring to himself, also understand them in the same sense of the words as David spoke them? That is, h—

<summary>This page concludes a physiological argument for the certainty of Jesus's death based on blood loss and physical trauma, then transitions into a theological inquiry regarding whether Jesus consciously identified himself as the subject of Davidic prophecy.</summary>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the identity of Jesus in relation to Jewish expectations; the catchword "to" (zu) suggests a transition into the fulfillment of prophecy.</meta>

Did Jesus not only consider and present himself as a true human being, but also as the essential Son of God, and furthermore as Priest, Teacher, and King all at once? I cannot answer both questions other than with a "Yes." Hear my evidence from the speeches of Jesus, as the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note> have recorded them. But first, for the sake of clarity, let us merge these questions into the following proposition: Jesus interpreted the prophecies of David literally as applying to himself, or, in other words: Jesus understood and accepted the Davidic prophecies concerning the Messiah in the exact sense of the words as David wrote them down.

The Jews called the books of the Old Covenant <note>The Old Testament.</note> all together "the Scripture," or "the Scriptures"; which therefore includes not only the books of Moses and the 16 Prophets, but also the Psalms, Matthew 21:42. Now Jesus asserts that these Scriptures testify of him, John 5:39. Thus, in this assertion of Jesus lies the following proposition: the Psalms bear witness to my person, to my office, suffering, death, resurrection, ascension, and glory. It is remarkable that Jesus uses the very language here which is put into the mouth of the Messiah in the prophecy of Psalm 40:8 <note>original: "Ps. 40, 8." In modern Bibles, this is often cited as Psalm 40:7.</note>:
->In the scroll of the book it is written of me.<-

In a similar way, the Jews called all the writings of the Old Testament "the Law and the Prophets," understanding by the former the five books of Moses, and by the latter the remaining holy books. Jesus makes use of this very linguistic custom, Luke 24:25, 27, compared with verse 44. Now, when he declares that he has come [to fulfill] the Law and the Prophets

Since the Psalms of David were also counted among these writings of the prophets, everything that Jesus taught here concerning the writings of all the prophets applies to them as well.

Jesus cites various prophecies of David with express words and explains them as referring to himself. Especially he showed them from the Psalms that he must have suffered, died, and risen again, and that he would have his teaching proclaimed among all nations, starting from Jerusalem, according to these prophecies, Luke 24:44–47.

Had Jesus presented himself as an adopted son of God—in the sense that every pious person can bear this name—he would have done nothing extraordinary by it; indeed, he would not have needed to say this, for his enemies could convict him of neither error nor fault; they mostly had to confess themselves that he was a pious man. — Had Jesus not confessed himself to be the essential Son of God, then his disciples, if they wished to remain honorable people, could not have presented him as such either; for they taught what they had heard, 1 John 1:1–3, compared with John 1:14. — Had Jesus considered himself an adopted son of God, like any other pious human being, he would not have been able to consider himself the person of whom David spoke. For David describes the Messiah as a person exalted above all men, as true God. *) Now, however, according to the passages from the speeches of Jesus cited at the beginning of this chapter, we must believe that Jesus entirely considered and presented himself as the person of whom David spoke; consequently, he must also have considered himself the Son of God who received his divine nature directly from God.

Let us now look more closely at some **passages of Scripture** in which **Jesus** presented himself as the **Son of God**. Once he spoke <note>original: "sprach er *)" referring to the footnote for John 9:35</note> to a man whom he had freed from his misery: **Do you believe in the Son of God?** This man answered: **Lord, who is he, so that I may believe in him? Jesus** said: **You see him here; I, who speak with you, am he.** The man replied: **Lord, I believe.** And he worshipped him. Would **Jesus** have been able to direct this man toward faith in him if he were not **God**? Faith is the only way in which man can honor **God** with his intellect; for to believe in someone who is not **God** is to commit **idolatry** <term>Abgötterey: the worship of idols or false gods</term>. **Jesus** has therefore either committed a **blasphemy** here—if, as a mere human, he directed faith toward his own person and also accepted the outward honor of **worship**—and we shall surely acquit **Jesus**, who honored **God** above all things, of this most gross crime—or else, we must confess that **Jesus** here presented himself as true **God**. **St. John 12:44, 45.**

Another time **Jesus** spoke <note>original: "spricht Jesus **)" referring to John 10:30</note>: **I and the Father are one.** That one must consider this not as a spiritual union, such as is common to all the godly, but as an essential union <term>wesentliche Vereinigung: a union of essence or substance, implying they are of the same divine nature</term>, is taught very clearly by **Jesus** in the continuation of this discourse; partly by directing his enemies to his miracles as the works of his Father, and partly through a double explanation of those first words <note>original: "***)" referring to verses 36 and 38</note>: **I am God's Son; the Father is in me, and I in him.** Compare these words also with **John 5:17.**

The author of the **Horus Right** <note>Referring to "The Rights of Horus," a rationalist work by Andreas Riem (1791) that attacked traditional Christology</note> is mistaken when he claims that Jesus believed himself, as the **Messias**, to have come directly from **God** (or, was born of Him, as expressed in the following words). What else could Jesus believe about his divine nature? How else could he have considered himself the Lord of **David**? According to his human nature, Jesus could not have believed that he came directly from **God**, that is, descended from heaven. For he was born of **Mary**, whom he loved and esteemed as his mother at all times, and for whom he, as a son, showed the most tender care even while on the cross; he expressly calls himself a **Man**, **Matthew 4:4**, and most preferably the **Son of Man**, as he was named in **Psalm 8:5**; he calls the **Christians** his brothers, **John 20:17**, as was prophesied in **Psalm 22:23**, and also speaks very often of himself as a human being—for example, when he prophesies about his suffering and death, **Luke 18:31 f.**, when he laments the anguish of his soul, and when he commits his spirit into the hands of God. — Since Jesus held himself to be and presented himself as a true human being (as was in accordance with the prophecies of **David**), how can the author of **Horus** claim that Jesus denied his mother? That he did not consider **Mary** to be his mother? That is certainly not contained in this speech of Jesus, where he proves himself to be the Lord of **David** according to his divine nature based on **Psalm 110:1**? It is utterly perverse when the author of ***Horus*** <note>The author refers to "Horus," a polemical work of the late 18th-century German Enlightenment that questioned the foundations of Christianity.</note> demands that Christians prove to him that Jesus did not consider David to be his Lord. They will certainly refrain from proving such a thing! And indeed, they cannot. But Christians can demonstrate to the author of ***Horus***, if he cares to know, that Jesus considered David to be his great-ancestor; that is to say: that Jesus believed he was a descendant of David.

— The Apostles proclaimed what they had seen of Jesus and what they had heard from him (1 John 1:3). These men assert, however, that Jesus was descended from the family of David (Matthew 1:1, 18; Luke 1:27, 32; Acts 13:22–23). Therefore, these people must either be saying what they heard from Jesus—namely, that he presented himself as a descendant of David—or they are deceiving us and not saying what they heard. The author of ***Horus*** himself acquits the Apostles of all deceit; consequently, they must have heard from the mouth of Jesus himself that he acknowledged himself as a Son of David. — How often was Jesus called the Son of David? That Canaanite woman (Matthew 15:22), that blind man near Jericho (Luke 18:38–39), that great multitude that received him solemnly, along with the children in the temple (Matthew 21:9, 15); everyone calls him the Son of David. That the prophecies of David concerning the Messiah were interpreted as referring to Jesus can only be unknown to those who have never laid eyes upon their writings. I would therefore be undertaking a superfluous task if I wished to demonstrate this at great length. However, so that one may more clearly perceive the strength of the proof—which we shall draw from David’s prophecies for the majesty of Jesus’s person and his Messiahship—the testimony of these teachers, who were directly sent and enlightened by God, must not be passed over in silence. I will therefore endeavor to show, from only a few passages in the writings of the Apostles, that they explained David’s prophecies as referring to Jesus, and publicly taught that this man, their Lord and Master, Jesus, is the person in whom all the words of David in the prophetic Psalms (which I have cited above; for I have restricted myself to these) have been fulfilled.

When Jesus was nailed to the cross, the Roman soldiers divided his garments among themselves and cast lots for his tunic. Indisputably, this circumstance—insignificant in itself—must have seemed very important to the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, because all four of them recount it. Why might it have seemed important to them? Without any doubt, it was because they saw therein the prophecy of David concerning the Messiah in Psalm 22:19 <note>In modern Bibles, this is usually cited as Psalm 22:18.</note> reaching its most exact fulfillment. Matthew expressly adds *): **That it might be fulfilled which was spoken by the prophet: They part my garments among them,** etc.

The messengers <note>apostles</note> of Jesus consistently maintain that their Lord was a true human being. For example, Paul not only explicitly calls him **man**, 1 Timothy 2:5, but also proves in Hebrews 2:12, 14, using Psalm 22:23, that he was born with a human nature just like other children. Paul’s proof is this: The Messiah already declares himself to be a man in the 22nd Psalm, as he did not consider it a disgrace to call men his brothers. The Messiah, therefore, did not assume a human nature—according to which he was named Jesus—merely in appearance, but in truth. This was so that he would be capable of dying, and through his death, might abolish the power of him who, as the deceiver of mankind, plunged them into death as the punishment for sin. Regarding humanity, however, he established a perfect redemption; otherwise, they would have been subjected, as it were, to a constant servitude <term>Knechtschaft: a state of slavery or spiritual bondage</term>, unable to have a joyful, confident heart, but instead always remaining mournful and despondent.

This same apostle speaks in Hebrews 2:6 and following, from Psalm 8:5 and following, regarding the dual state of Jesus. His lecture is briefly this: One must not use the lowliness of Jesus as an objection against his majesty, but rather one must remember his exaltation; for David connects both with one another when he envisions the Messiah as an initially despised man, but thereafter as a person elevated to the highest glory. David called upon his nation to expect just such an exalted teacher. — Jesus Christ came into the world; this is the expression used in Psalm 40:8: *Behold, I come*. — Jesus had the willing desire to enlighten all people; not only to impart a soul-saving knowledge of God to the Jews, but to make the name of the Lord known to an even larger congregation, Jews and Gentiles alike (Matthew 28:19, Mark 16:15), according to the prophecy in Psalm 22:23, 26.

Paul proves the universal royal dominion of Jesus in 1 Corinthians 15:27 and Hebrews 2:8 from Psalm 8:7. In the same way, he demonstrates Jesus's participation in the shared dominion with God—even over the enemies of the Messiah—in 1 Corinthians 15:25 from Psalm 110:1, and cites this as the reason for the Christians' fearlessness before God in Romans 8:34. — The kingdom of Jesus is described by the apostles as a spiritual and heavenly one (Romans 14:17, 18). ## The Seventh Chapter.

### The strength of the proof for the divine majesty and the Messiahship of Jesus, drawn from the prophecies of David.

I come now to the final part of my treatise, where I shall seek to utilize the principal points I have hitherto stated and proven, and to show how strongly and convincingly the prophecies of David prove the divinity and the <term>Messiahship</term> <note>German: Messiat; the office or status of being the Messiah.</note> of Jesus, the crucified and risen one.

The author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to "Fragments of the Wolfenbüttel Unnamed," specifically the work "On the Intention of Jesus and His Disciples" published by Lessing, which sparked the Fragmentenstreit or "Fragments Controversy."</note> must himself confess <margin>Preface, page 4.</margin> that a most striking agreement is to be found between the prophecies of the prophets (consequently also those of David) and those events which occurred with Jesus. Now the question is: from whence did those prophecies and these fulfillments of the same in Jesus arise? Either

<summary>This page transitions from scriptural definitions of Christ's spiritual kingdom to a formal defense of Jesus's divinity based on the prophetic writings of King David, specifically addressing contemporary skeptical critiques.</summary>
<keywords>Kingdom of God, Messiahship, Prophecy, King David, Divinity of Christ, Horus Fragment, Biblical fulfillment</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the impossibility of chance in the fulfillment of prophecy; the catchword "wiss" (knowledge/certainty) connects to the final sentence here.</meta>

Either both arose from mere chance, or both were contemplated by a wise Understanding—the former made known by God, and the latter carried out by Him with infinite power. That David, by mere coincidence or through blind chance, wrote down all these events which occurred more than a thousand years after him to a single person, is unthinkable; it contradicts itself. That all those circumstances could have united in Jesus as punctually as David had recorded them, by mere chance, is just as unthinkable and just as absurd as the former. The dreams of Democritus and Epicurus <note>Ancient Greek philosophers who proposed that the universe was formed by the random collision of atoms.</note> regarding a blind chance have long since ceased to be believed by any rational person, because this "chance" is in itself a non-entity.

If David had stumbled upon the thoughts he wrote down by chance; if that which David had recorded had been fulfilled so precisely in Jesus by mere accident, then there would be more found in the effect than in the cause. There must, therefore, have been an acting cause that is superhuman <note>original: "übermenschlich"</note> both in the prophecies of David and in the punctual fulfillment of the same in Jesus (See 2 Peter 1:20-21 <note>A New Testament passage stating that no prophecy of Scripture comes from one’s own interpretation, but from God.</note>). In the former, the cause ensured they became known to David; in the latter, it ordered the circumstances so that they coincided with that revelation. 

Aside from God, we cannot conceive of this acting cause; consequently, it is God Himself who—to speak of Him in human terms—contemplated a plan which He made known to David and carried out in Jesus. — The all-wise God does nothing without achieving the most important purposes. Could one imagine that He revealed insignificant trifles to David? No! It must have been of the utmost importance, that which God [made known] in advance to His servant—

know... <note>Catchword "wiss" likely leading to "wissen ließ" (let know) or "Wissenschaft" (knowledge).</note>

made it known. Should He have carried out these previously announced things upon Jesus without achieving the most important ultimate purpose? Certainly not! He would have acted contrary to His perfections. —
What important matter would it have been, then, if Jesus were a mere human and His Messiahship <note>original: "Messiat"</note> of no entirely special nature? If He, like any other human, were meant to rule, teach, suffer, and die? If Jesus were a mere human, then God, through these prophecies and their fulfillment, did more than a mortal man—who can boast of no higher origin than other men—is worthy of. If the death of Jesus was the death of a common man, then God could have had no ultimate purpose at all in the prior announcement of it. Have not other righteous people also been killed? Did God carry out an important work through that? If Jesus were nothing more than a teacher of morality, how could God refer people to Him, when those men <note>likely referring to the prophets or philosophers</note> who were just as great moralists could have promoted God’s purpose just as well? Had this been His purpose in having prophecies made about Jesus, it would have been too insignificant. Should Jesus have been an earthly king of the Jews, it would hardly have been worth the effort for God to have prophesied about this king and his kingdom a thousand years beforehand and to have let these prophecies be fulfilled. For the intentions that God wishes to achieve through a worldly monarch, He can achieve through any king. Why would He not also have had prophecies made concerning David and Solomon? — In a word: if the person and the Messiahship of Jesus had not been of an entirely unique nature, God would have acted contrary to His perfection by making great preparations through the prophecies and

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the divine origin of Davidic prophecies. This page continues the argument regarding God's ultimate purpose in fulfilling those prophecies through Jesus.</meta>

...would have allowed these to be fulfilled in such a striking manner without thereby carrying out important ultimate purposes.

God is the cause that enabled David to prophesy, and the cause for these prophecies to be fulfilled in Jesus. In both cases, He acted both indirectly <note>German: mittelbar; through natural means or human agency</note> and directly <note>German: unmittelbar; through supernatural intervention</note>; consequently, the ultimate purpose He intended to achieve through this must be exceedingly important. As an infinitely merciful God, He is deeply concerned with the welfare of mankind: consequently, in the prophecies of David and their fulfillment in Jesus, He must have simultaneously had the welfare of mankind as His objective. Indeed, this welfare of mankind must have been one of God's most prominent ultimate purposes (for God achieves several purposes at once) in the fulfillment of David's prophecies that occurred in Jesus. Why else would all of this have been made known and fulfilled among men by God? If God had merely intended the promotion of the physical and temporal welfare of men as His purpose, then the preparations for it would have been greater than the purpose itself. Consequently, the merciful God must have set a far more important purpose as His objective, namely the eternal welfare of mankind. Without achieving this ultimate purpose, it would be unworthy of God to have acted both indirectly and directly. — Now, who does not see that God could have achieved little or nothing for the eternal happiness of mankind if Jesus had been a mere mortal like us, if He had been a teacher like the pagan teachers, or if He had been a king like other worldly monarchs? The morality which reason teaches is, when correctly recognized, good and ex-
cellent,

The praise that he gives to God on the one hand, he robs from Him on the other. It would likely be no great praise for a king if one said of him: He tolerates and brings happiness to robbers, murderers, rebels, and all manner of dissolute rabble in his kingdom, because he is far too great to be offended. <meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological inquiry into the nature of divine justice and the necessity of a mediator, following the previous discussion on the limitations of human virtue.</meta>

Should people meet the same fate? Those who practiced an imperfect virtue, incapable of receiving God's extraordinary rewards, and those who were entirely consumed by vice? Shall they both be overtaken by the misery that naturally and necessarily follows from evil deeds? If so, then no one at all could be extraordinarily rewarded by God. — But is there no possibility of becoming free from these sad consequences of evil deeds? Shall we try to make amends for the evil through good deeds? In how few cases are we able to do so! And even assuming we could, would our conscience then fall completely silent? Is our virtue not forever branded by a single evil deed? Will our conscience conceal this from us? Will it not always hold before us the agonizing thought: "This good that you did was your duty, and it does not justify that evil"? Therefore, there is no means within our own power to undo the evil we have committed. Meanwhile, one often sees that people are freed from the bad consequences of their ill conduct before worldly courts—just as debtors are released from prisons through the bail of their friends; should this be impossible before God? This would, of course, have to be a more than human person, an extraordinary surety. But where do we find him? — Behold! David pointed to such a perfectly virtuous person, who was to suffer and die for the sake of others' transgressions. Has a fanatic <note>original: "Schwärmer" – a term often used in the 18th century to describe religious enthusiasts or deluded visionaries</note> or an impostor ever been able to perform divine miracles, or to predict future accidental events with certainty?

But did the miracles of Jesus actually happen? Were they divine or natural wonders? These are two questions that deserve an investigation. Regarding the first, it is historically certain that Jesus performed miracles. Friends and enemies of Jesus testify to their certainty. He did not perform them in a corner, but publicly before the eyes of very many witnesses. The Apostles wrote down the accounts of these miracles of Jesus at the time when the Jews and heathens <note>original: "Heiden" – non-Christians, specifically Greeks and Romans in this context</note>, whom they wished to make into disciples of Jesus, could inquire exactly into the truth of the matter, and indeed did so inquire. For the many learned and wise men, who became Christians through the instruction of the Apostles shortly after the Ascension of Jesus, would surely have inquired exactly into the matter before they accepted a doctrine that was contrary to their prevailing evil inclinations, and upon the adoption of which they had to cast off their favorite sins. Could these men not have learned with little effort whether Jesus had performed such miracles as the Apostles claimed? Would they have accepted this doctrine if the Apostles had dealt in untruths? The Apostles gave and authenticated their testimony before Jewish and heathen courts, according to all the strictness of the laws and the right; if their claim regarding the miracles of Jesus had been unfounded, would they not have been contradicted by a thousand witnesses? The Jews themselves could not deny the miracles of Jesus, but sought, in their enmity against Jesus, to help themselves by attributing these miracles to an
evil <note>The text cuts off here; the next page likely continues with "spirit" or "principle."</note>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Jesus's miracles against skeptics, specifically addressing historical testimonies from both Jewish and Pagan sources.</meta>

attributed it to an evil spirit, an opinion which refutes itself. For Jesus was a friend of God, and an enemy of all evil; his miracles were nothing but acts of charity; can an act of charity spring from an evil spirit? Even the Talmud testifies <margin>See Eisenmenger’s *Judaism Unmasked*, Part 1, page 261 and following. <note>Johann Andreas Eisenmenger (1654–1704) was a German Orientalist; his work was a famous, though highly controversial and polemical, collection of Jewish texts.</note></margin> that the miracles of Jesus occurred; and as much as modern Jews find themselves troubled by this testimony, they still do not dare to strike it out or declare it to be untrue. Should this testimony from the sworn enemies of Jesus not be considered important? Even the enemies of the Christian religion among the heathens did not venture to deny the miracles of Jesus. Celsus, a philosopher <note>original: "Weltweiser" (world-wise man)</note> who lived about 100 years after Jesus, traveled to the Jewish land himself to investigate the nature of Christianity; and this sharp opponent of the divine doctrine by no means denies that Jesus performed miracles. Not long after him lived Porphyry, who, although he wrote against the Christian religion, did not contest the miracles of Jesus. Hierocles, a very distinguished man from among the heathens who lived around the year 300 after Jesus Christ, likewise admits that Jesus performed miracles.

Thus, friends and enemies, the learned and the unlearned, all testify that the miracles of Jesus truly happened. The author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to the "Wolfenbüttel Fragments" or similar Enlightenment-era skeptical works often personified or titled "Horus" in contemporary polemics.</note> likely knew this; he therefore did not dare to argue against the strongest historical evidence; however, because the history of Jesus's miracles would have dealt a fatal blow to his doctrinal structure, he sought to attack these miracles from another side, so that they might be less harmful to his system.

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's systematic defense of the supernatural nature of Jesus's miracles against a rationalist critic. The previous context likely established the first question regarding the authenticity of the miracles; here, the author moves to the second question regarding their origin.</meta>

This now concerns my second question, namely: whether the miracles of Jesus were divine or natural? My opponent thus declares the miracles of Jesus to be entirely natural things; however, his explanations are—as is not otherwise to be expected—so absurd that they refute themselves. In this, he often proceeds so "skillfully" <note>The author uses irony here to mock the opponent's logic.</note> that one cannot help but laugh. A few examples will demonstrate this. The miracle where Jesus fed more than 5000 people with 5 barley loaves, he explains thus *): These people had taken enough bread with them for the journey; they therefore received the small slices of bread from the disciples merely as "tidbits," and actually ate their fill from their own bread baskets. This quite "nicely" devised explanation I wish to recommend to all hypochondriacs <note>In the 18th century, "hypochondria" referred to a general state of melancholy or digestive malaise; the author suggests the absurdity of the argument is funny enough to aid digestion.</note> to read after dinner as a good means of promoting digestion. 

Yet a few small questions: Were these people on a journey, or were they merely accompanying Jesus from the nearest places? How does one know that they had enough bread with them? Were they all friends of Jesus, or for the most part merely curious onlookers, who might not have considered the bread slices as mere tidbits? Were the loaves so large that they could be divided into so many slices? <meta>This page continues the defense of Jesus's miracles and prophecies against rationalist critiques, specifically addressing the arguments found in the "Horus" (likely referring to the "Wolfenbüttel Fragments" or similar Enlightenment-era skepticism).</meta>

The opinion is, as it is said in the schools, a *presumption of the marvelous* <note>original: "praesumtio mirabilitatis"; a scholastic term for the biased assumption that an event must be supernatural</note>, and indeed one driven to the highest degree. Why should one accept this opinion? Is there cause for it? Can it be supported by reasons? No! It is an opinion devised with enough partisanship and the worst malice to dispute the honor of Jesus: how irrationally one would act if one chose to accept it!

From these examples, one will recognize how futile the objections against the miracles of Jesus are, and how foolish it is when one tries to derive them from natural causes. It remains certain, therefore, that Jesus performed divine miracles; and if this is so, we are bound to believe the testimony of Jesus—that he is the divine person depicted by David in his prophecies.

However, as I said above, Jesus is also to be cleared of all fanaticism <term>Schwärmerey: a 18th-century pejorative for religious delusion or irrational enthusiasm</term> and deception because of his prophecies. Against the prophecies of Jesus, the author of the *Horus* <note>A reference to "Adonis und Horus," a skeptical work by Karl Friedrich Bahrdt or similar contemporary deist literature</note> knew nothing important to bring forward except this: that Jesus had taken them from the Old Testament; for example, from Daniel, the destruction of the city of Jerusalem and its Temple. Let us suppose the case that Jesus knew the main circumstances of future events from the Old Testament; did he not prophesy because of that? Did he not define those circumstances more precisely, and make known in advance several details that would likewise manifest in the future events? Did Daniel, for example, name the specific generation <note>original: "Menschengeschlecht," here meaning the contemporary generation of people</note> under which the Temple in Jerusalem was to be destroyed? Did he say that this Temple, after its destruction,
